THE SAME AGAIN.

Trades Council Rename Messrs. Dukes And Seahury

FOR WAR PENSIONS COMMITTEE.

Despite the fact that Mr. Chas. Dukes is now a ,member of His Majesty's Forces, his name and that of Mr. Seabury have again been put forward/ by the Trades and Labour Council for membership on the War Pensions Committee. It will be remembered that although those gentlemen, among others, were selected 133; the delegates of trade unions to represent Labour on the committee, the Town Council refused to accept them owing to their anti-war views, and the factors in the deadlock werefubMitted to tha Statutory Committee in London for solution. That committee's suggestions were contained in the following letter sent ,on September 4th and read to the Trades Council on , Tuesday evening: "I am directed by the War Pensions, B:c., Statutory Committee to refer to the

recommendation made of Mr. Dukes and Mr. Seabury for appointment as members of the Warrington Local Committee and to the- objec- tion made by the Town Council to the recom- mendation. lam to say that the -Statutory Committee understand that Mr. Dukes has been called up for military service, and, having failed to report himself, has been handed or to a military escort. In these circumstances the committee' apprehend apprehend that a fresh, 4,ecom- mendation will be made in place of Mr. Dukes, atd as a meeting of the trade unions and the Trades and Labour Council will be necessary for this purpose, it is suggested that in order to avoid any further difficulty it would he desirable if the meeting would reconsider the recommendation of Mr. Seabury." Upon this an interesting discussion enpued, the ultimate being that the Council decided to take no heed of the hint.

Town Council " Don't Like" Mr. Seabury. The Chairman (Mr. Edwin Plinston) re- marked that as a result of the deadlock and the vacancies in the Labour representation there was bo,und • to be 'another conference Of trade unionists, and the question for that meeting to decide was what policy they should adopt. The Statutory Committee had droppolil Mr. Dukes altogether, and suggested that the trade unions should drop 'Mr. Seabury. Mr. Royle: For what reason? The Chairman: Well, because the Town Council don't like him. Mr. Royle : The letter seems to admit that the 'Town Council have the power to appoint these men.

The Chairman: And yet it does not. They are 'very careful to say _only that it would be desirable for the meeting to reconsider the recommendation.

Mr. Seabury pointed. out that as both the delegates who attended the last conference were now members of His Majesty's Forces it would be necessary to appoint two more. • Mr. J. Hamblett remarked that Mr. Dukes had been placed out of Court altogetlitr, but they had a right to maintain their nomination of Mr..Seabury. He was of opinion that they should not go behind that at all, and he was prepared to stand up for it—indeed, he felt they ought to take the matter up with the Labour Party.. In some way or other, ths Statutory Committee had been biased right from the be- ginning. That was the general feeling. The committee might think they had been impaitial, but a great deal of correspondence must have passed with them from certain quarters; other- wise they would not be *so well primed as to the turn of events. He had been in conversation with several people who were not usually in sympathy with tile Trades Council, but who were far from satAfied wilb the attitude which had been taken up with 'Fegard to Labour on the new committee.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
A Principle Involved.

Mr. Seabury regarded the matter purely frona the point of view of principle. It was not .a question of Seabury, Dukes or any other man. As far as he personally was concerned, he was not particular whether he got a seat on the committee or not. He had enough work to do for. nothing. (Laughter.) At the same time, the trade unionists of Warrington had met on two Occasions and definitely-asserted that certain individuals should represent them on the War Pensions Committee. It appeared to him that the Labour movement in Warrington was suffi- ciently intelligent to select the men most likely to serve Labour's best interests, rather than allow the people on the other side to 'select the representatives for 'them. Mr. Bell suggested that they should invite the Town Council to come down and select the representatives. The meeting laughed at his satire.

Messrs. F. Berry and J. Hamblett were ap- pointed to represent the Trades Council at any further conference that might be called, Mr. Seabary having intimated that he would prefer to attend as representing his own union, that of the General Labourers, and Gas Workers. In the course of further discussion it was opined by Mr. Seabury, that the real work of the committee would in all probability begin when the war was over, whereupon Mr. Bell remarked that by that time Mr. Dukes would probably have been liberated from military duties, and would be in a position to attend the meetings of the committee. The best policy, .therefore, would be to adhere to the nomination of Messrs. Dukes and Seabury. 'Mr. Berry, however, asked that, as the war might last two years, were they to wait that long before the committee began to pay pen- sions? -

Mr. H. Harrison: Officers can attend to their Parliamentary duties, so Ntrhy can't others? Dukes isn't going to be in the Army two years, if he is dealt with as a conscientious objector should be, for long before then he should .be referred to work of national_ importance, and could attend to this work. If anyone of the Labour men has to be knocked off let the whole lot resign. It is a matter of principle, and we should adhere to our previous nominations. Later in the discussion, the Chairman re- marked that if he were a soldier, injured and wanting a pension, he would rather have his case judged by men of the calibre of the Trades Council nominees, who knew that it was to work for a miserable pittance, and in their time had felt the pinch of poverty, rather than by men who were holding positions in the War- rington Town Council, and who had probably never experienced that condition. ,Mr. G. Chamberlain moved that the Council reaffirm its decision that Messrs. Dukes and Seabury be elected representatives on the com- mittee, or that they appoint tb representatives at all, and Mr. Bell seconded. Mr. T. Howard said that if Messrs. Dukes and Seabury were not able to represent them on the committee, he, without any detriment to others on the Council, did not think thereivas anyone else qualified to undertake the task.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Seabury v. Councillor Roberts.

Remarking that Councillor Roberts had de- clared at the Trades Council did not repre- sent the trade unions of Warrington, Mr. Seabury took occasion to offer a challenge' to that gentleman. "I say to Mr. Roberts," Mr. Seabury said, "that I am prepared to submit his name and mine to the trade unionists of Warrington and see whom they will select for this committee—or any other Labour reptesen- tative, it does not matter who he' is. Mr. Roberts may probably 'accept the challenge. I hope he does. The men for whom we are working know that we are acting in their own interests and would support us in this Matter when they are not poisoned on the political

While not Canting it to be thought that he was throwing Mr. Dukes overboard—he was the last mania-the world to, do that—Mr. Hamblett said they shottld consider the other side of the mattyr. If there was .a possibility of Mr. Dukes being able to attend the meetings of the committee, he would be quite in favour of press- ing for his election. He ,was, however, anxious that they should not jeopardise their opportu- nity of working in the inteests of the soldiers and sailors who would conie before the com- mittee. Those men on the committee-who were in direo opposition to Labour might not deal with the cases in as generous a spirit as was to be desired.

The resolution was carried with only one dis- sentient, and it was also agree 4 that if their nominations were not accepted by the Town Council they should take no further part in the selection of Labour representatives.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 16, 1916.

KITCHENER'S LETTER.

BAPTIST V. WESLEYAN.

Famous Document And Portrait The Rev. H. Ogden Replies To At Town Hall. The Rev. H. T. Dixon.

WHERE WERE THE , PEOPLE ?

Warringtonians were afforded opportunity for hero worship and charity at the Town Hall on Saturday, but disappointingly few paid their tribute. 'The occasion was the exhibition of the famous letter which the late Lord Kitchener addressed to the nation appealing for 300,000 men; and also the striking portrait by Herkomer—these his- torical objects being on view to aid the British Red Cross -Society. •Withal, only 240 people— not a company in a battalion—diverted them- selves to 'the scene; the admission takings total- ling only £4 ss. Several, scores oi those who attended were wounded soldiers. Warrington has, perhaps, never behaved so shabbily before, and when we match against this sum the .4370 raised by a similar entrance fee in another town, we feel almost ashamed. The exhibition was advertised by means of the local Press, streamers across Market Gate and the Park Gates, and a gigantic Union Jack in front of the Town Hall. Most people knew of it, yet they did not come—a most curious abstention. Happily, when the letter was copied and posted on the walls last year more noticc was taken of it.

4 To those who re-read the letter fastened in a becoming glass case, it was extremely fascinat- ing; it was so direct in its phrasing, strong in its patriotism, and convincing in its appeal. Everyone knows its message, but it was thrill- ing to imagine its writer seated at the War Office making draft after draft, improving it here and then there, and finally handing the finished product—which we now saw ourselves— to the printer and, through him, to the whole nation. By the recruiting authorities It is 01- mated that the publication of the letter in the newspapers and on: the placards stimulated re- cruiting more than any other poster they had issued. And here was the actual writing. The next time we wish to see it we shall have to go to the British Museum.

By the side of the letter and overshadowing it in siie and 'approaching it in interest, stood the wen,derful portrait by Herkomer. With the sand of Egypt and Mohammedan ,tombs as a background, Kitchener makes an 'impressive figure. Painted in 1890, when he was a Colonel in the Royal Engineers, and had yet to make the Empire ring with enthusiasm at his name, he looks more lithe than in-later years, but the strong, clear-cut features, the searching and far- seeing blue eyes• (as of one given to meditation and action), and the kindly expression pre- dominate as when he took the helm in August, 1914. The next time we wish to see this picture we .shall have to go to the National Gallery. What an experience all but the 240 have missed!

===== 0002601_19160916_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
A HAPPY AUGURY UNFULFILLED.

The opening ceremony was presided over bY Miss Sylvia Broadbent (president of the Dares- bury Dividion of the 8.R.C.5.), in the presence of a considerable gathering, which seemed to

augur—but did not—popularity for the exhibi- tion. '

Declaring the exhibition, open, the ,Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) said: "Lord Kitchener, I think, was the most illustrious -soldier Eng- land ever had. It appears to me somewhat 'of a coincidence that to-day is Lifeboat .Flag Day in Warrington, for if ever we had a lifeboat. in England in a military sense it was when Lord Kitchener stepped' into the breach at a- time when his services were so urgently- needed. (Applause.) In the letter which we see before us he asked for a very small number of recruits —300,000; _Perhaps he little thought at that time that so many would rally round the flag in order to uphold British traditions. " ALLEGED " PACIFICISM.

The congregation at the Golborne-street Baptist Church had a rare experience on Sun- day evening of hearing a sermon delivered in reply to a minister of a different denomination, the Rev._ H. Ogden taking \ exception to the utterances of Rev. H. T.o.Dixon in Penketh Wesleyan Church a fortnight previously. , It will be remembered that the Rev. H. T. Dixon iS "anti-war." Ever since he had breathed Baptist atmo- sphere, declared the ' Rev. H. Ogden, he had joined the forces to establish a world-peace, and it was not easy' for him two years ago to for- sake his attitude; in fact, it would have been I much easier to have adopted the 'alleged " pacifist" views which were held by the Rev. H. T. Dixon and others. But he felt that Eng- land was justified in establishing the freedom of smaller peoples by the Sword. He had the sincerest regard for Mr. Dixon, and, on frequent occasions, had crossed swords with him a in privSe, and whatever might be now said was not in any sort of dislike to him, but merely of his opinions; in fact, he had the highest respect for the man himself. ,

===== 0002601_19160916_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
CONFUSED ISSUES.

The reverend gentleman had taken very strong exception to the attitude of the Church, on the one hand, to brutal sport, and, on the other, to its interest in the war. If he (the preacher) did not know Mi. Dixon so well he should think him to be guilty of the greatest levity, for he was riiclucing the whole international conflift to so thuch burlesque. In brutal sport there was no moral question involved, but in this war there was. That accounted for the difference in the attitude of the Churches. Mr. Dixon, who knew the majority of his colleague,: in the Churches, would surely deem, their con- victions to be only actuated by the deepest or moral motives, yet, on one occasion his spleen overcame him and ,he threw a jibe at them be- cause they had not been included in the Mili- tary Service Act. Surely, he must know that his colleagues would only take the stand they had done in the support of the war in the highest of principles, and with preparedness to suffer as much for their convictions' as the reverend gentleman was ready to suffer for his.

WOULD CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTORS SUBMIT?

It was all very well for people to speak of thi, sth 'Chapter of Matthew regarding their con- duct. Not five per cent. of the conscientious objectors would return a kiss for a blow as they were commanded'. He (the preacher) admitted that the whole groundwork of Christian ethics was individual. They, might be smitten and cruelly abused, but they had got to bear it for the Lord. But he would point crut that when they passed from the individual to the com- munal sense there was a great difference. Was there,a man who remained such a pacifist that he would apply the sth of Matthew to his wife, child and mother, and stand aside while his wife was polluted, his child torn ,limb from limb, his grey-haired mother treated with con- tempt and cruelty? No! He believNl 'his Gospel to say to him that he should do all he could to prevent that. He (the preacher) also believed in conscience, not regarding it as an anomaly as some people did.

SWORD THE GUARDIAN OF FREEDOM.

But the rea-on that there was freedom of conscience at all was because there had been a man with -a sword in this land of ours. It was Oliver Cromwell and the Puritan Ironsides who fought for conscience. Was it wrong that any- one should fight for,the right? Who would say that the North was wrong in fighting for the freedom of the Southern slaves in the American Civil War? Therefore, was it wrong that we should fight on behalf of smaller peoples Who were trampled upon byl powerful States? Mr: Dixon ?oust Temember that besides there being men with consciences who stayed behind there were men with consciences who went forward to the firing line. The men who had gone from that Church had been inspired by the highest

FOR CONSCIENCE' SAKE.

Wives Deny That There Has Been Application For Relief.

To the Editor of " The Examiner."

Sir,—Regarding the report in your paper, headed "For Conscience' Sake," we take it that we are the wives of the conscientious objectors referred to,, and as such we would ask that you will 'allow us the opportunity to clear up what appears to be a misunderstanding. We have proof that our husbands have not asked for relief toibe granted to their wives; it is diffi- cult to understand" how the Board of Guardians could possibly have received word from the two conscientious objectors asking that relief to be granted. The report as set out in your paper is altogether misleading, and certainly not a correct statement of facts.

Trusting that you will give this letter the same publicity as you gave the report o the Board of Guardians meeting. Yours faithfully, HELEN DAVIES and IDA COOKE. Daisy Bank, Penketh, September 13, 1916. ,

Our report stated that two conscientious objectors who had been court-martialled had written requesting that the Board should afford relief to their wives and chil- dren. That paragraph should read that the two conscientious objectors referred to have alleged to the Home Office Committee of Employment of conscientious objectors 1h at if an allowance is not made to their wives and children they would be left in a position recessitating poor relief.

IN THE COURTS. 1

ANOTHER SEPARATION.

Husband Sent To Gaol FOr Two Months On Serious Charge.

Sydney Packer, of 35, Hesketh-street, was, at the Borough Police Court on Saturday, sen- tenced to two months' imprisonment for causing grievous bodily harm to his wife, Elizabeth, and for committing an aggravated assault on Catherine Evans, a neighbour. The Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls) explained that on the night of the Ist inst. the prisoner went home in a quarrelsome mood and commenced to call his •wife bad names. He picked up a knife and threatened to run it into her. He then struCk her with his fist and knocked her on tarthe flair and, while there, kicked her several times, causing her much pain. She was afterwards taken to the Infirm- ary, where she was detained for some days suffering from a fractured collar bone, bruises on the shoulder, and a cut on the head. Mrs. Evans heard`the noise and told the prisoner he I wag nothing but a, coward. He, thereupon, rushed at her with a poker and struck her on the forehead, causing bloot to flow freely. Corroborative evidence was given, P.C. Willis speaking as to arresting the accused, and stating that when charged with the offence against his wife, he replied, "I wish I had her."

Mr. T. S. Steel, who defended, said that the parties had lived together very unhappily and had once been separated. His wife, however, had returned to him. She had been in the habit of staying out tlate at night, and he had had L.) complain about her conduct to Inspector Wain- wright, of the N.S.P.C.C. The prisoner denied that on the night in question he used the poker as alleged.

SENT TO INDUSTRIAL SCHOOL.

A small boy was charged at the Juvenile Court on Wednesday with stealing a watch and chain valued 6s. 6d., the property of Annie Ridgway, 55, Howley-lane, ; and also with stealing, some time during the past fortnight, a second watch, valued 2s. 6d.

Detective Turner said the boy admitted that he went into the house, 55, Howley-lane, during the temporary absence of the tenant and took the watch, which was hanging over the mantel- shelf. The boy, continued the officer, seemed to be a thorough thief. . He had previously stolen a watch in Stockton Heath, a razor in Howley, and had pilfered wholesale from Ah 3 ma'rket. The head teacher of the school which the boy attended could say nothing good for him, and he was strongly suspected of stealing a shilling from the school some time ago. In committing him to an industrial school until he attained sixteen years of age—his father to pay three shillings a week for his mainten- ance—the Chairman of the Bench told the boy he was sorry to see him in such a position, and hoped he would make good when he left the school.

Railway, for having on the 16th August, as alleged, obstructed another railway official, Ticket-collector Wm. John Holland. , The case was heard at Thursday's Borough Court, Mr. Pride, solicitor, Euston, appearing for the railway company. _ _

• Evidence was given to the effect that defend- ant travelled by train from Blackpool to War- rington, arriving at /Bank Quay at 11.26 p.m. He walked past the ticket-collector, and when the latter followed him and asked for his ticket he said he had lost it, and refused to pay. He also pushed the collector asidP. The solici- tor pointed out that by defendant keeping the collector away from his post for some time a considerable lot of people got away without giving up their tickets. Defendant, in giving evidence on his own be- half, said that he told the ticket checker he could not find his ticket, and that he had taken

the number and would see about it in the morn- ing. He found the ticket when he got home and brought it down to the station the folloW- ing morning. The magistrates fined defendant ten shillings.

WORKING MEN'S CONCERT

ABANDONED.

Sir,—ln consequence of conditions beyond the tontrol of the Working Men's Infirmary Com- Inittee, they have decided to abandOn the annual concert held in aid of the funds of our hospital, and to appeal to the employes of the town and neighbourhood of Warrington for a Special contribution to meet the financial loss Which will be sustained. The war-time prices of provisions and other requirements have added materially to the cost of maintenance of the Institution. The year 1916 began with a debit batuice of £1,583 13s. 4d., which, it is estimated, will be increased at the close of the year to over .t 2,000. In terms of an arrangement entered into with the Military Authorities, 52 beds are reserved for sick and wounded soldiers, and \these beds are, at the present time, all occupied. We fully realise the exceptional calls which are being made on the charitably disposed, but, as the Infirmary, which is supported by volun- tary contributions, is so greatly in need of financialsupport, we venture most earnestly to request the kind help of our workpeople in the form of an increased subscription to our fonds,

GEORGE WALKER, Chairman. WM. DAINTITH, Vice-Chairman. J. H. J. HAMPSON, Hon., Secretary.

WOMEN LABOUR IN TANNERIES.

Sir,—At a recent sitting of the Warrington Tribunal one of the members commented upon the fact that the Orford Tannery wee an ex- ample to other employers in the manner in Which they released men for H.M. Forces, and replaced them by female labour.

Now this method has been adopted by the insiority of master, tanners in Warrington and district with general satisfaction, and the work ,has gone on quite smoothly, this notwithstand- !lig the fact that the tanners have instructions to maintain their regular output as their pro- duct is urgently required for the use of our forces and those of our Allies. At an abnormal time like the present the efforts of our womenfolk to fill the breach are - Properly appreciated, and Whilst the job they tackle is not very Congenial they are doing splendidly, cognisant of the fact that they, as Well as the men they have released, are doing their bit for King and, country in this great Btloiggle for liberty and right. , Further, tanners have, again notice to " un- 13adge .” many more of their men, and still there must be no 'diminution in ttieir output. In order to comply it will be necessary to in- crease the employment of women, and with these facts in mind, I, along with others, would like to know the reason why women labour has not been introduced into all tanneries, alid by this means release many young eligible men who Would be better employed in if tanning the cities" of the Huns.

The best thanks are due to the women who. When the boys sacrificed their position and 'torched away to do their duty, stepped into their places and kept the "ball rolling.", This they are still willing to do. and more if neces- -8,417, to bring this struggle to a satisfactory ebnclusion.

ERNEST C. SUTTON.

The Hawthorne. Weaste-lane, Thelwall.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
"EXCLUDED.. AND ISOLATED."

The Position Of Cairo Street Chapel

And Its Minister.

Sir,—Still a reader of your columns, I: ,have just come across the account given therein of the sermon "The Church Catholic," by the Rev. Irvine Lister. Leaving much of it to be dealt with as they may think fit by those whom it may more nearly concern, there is one Point in Mr. Lister's charge of universal exclu- sion and isolation which I would like, with YOur permission, to write a word upon.

*The." more or less informal gathering of Free Church ministers who month by month meet for help and fellowship" to which "the min- ister of Cairo-street Chapel is not invited, nor is his presence desired," is a body to which I acted as a kind of convener for some few years of the period of my ministry in Warrington. It Possessed a decidedly social side, while existing Inainly for the help derived from the inter- change of thought and experience in connec- tion with our work; and at that time I was exceedingly concerned for what I felt must be _the loneliness of Mr. Lister's predeceßsor, Dr. Mellor, and made an unsuccessful attempt to bring him within the fraternal circle. I did not feel inclined to fear the introduction of any Poisonous bacteria any more than I would pro- bably have done had the circle been enlarged

SUIT CASES,

===== 0002601_19160916_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
4

FOR TRAVELLING TRUNKS,

DRESS BASKETS, &c.,

===== 0002601_19160916_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
51,53 & 55, BRIDGE STREET, WARRINGTON.

so as to take within its perimeter clerical members of the Roman and Anglican Com- munions, which I wish it had. I failed, and still fail, to see any reason ,why Dr. Mellor should not have been admitted, and think I would have been prepared, should the circum- stances have arisen, either to give Or take a licking within the theological arena with as much sportily the average. 'ioind what I tried to do in the case of Dr. Mellor I have reason to know my reverend brother of Wycliffe tried" to accomplish on behalf of Mr. Lister. In fairness to, the brethren in the circle I do not think they were entirely responsible for the attitude taken up. No church, in my experience, in Warrington has any truer right to the term " Christian " than has Cairo-street. When Presbyterianism .was exceedingly low, Cairo-street, under the leadership of the late and respected • Rev. Mr. Harvey-Cook, went .out of its, way to render that assistance which, offere4 in that most psychological moment, enabled my former Church to catch the tide and live again.. The Spirit of -our Blessed Lord certainly expressed itself in that generous and continuous action. Nevertheless, I am afraid teat from the tail of the sermon there strikes the note which accounts for the undesirable position com- plained of. There is no doubt that the founder of Mr. Lister's congregation, who was a Presby- terian clergyman of the Established Church, and Rector of. Warrington, did stand for liberty of conscience, and of interpretation, but he Would never reconcile that with a statement that. " the idea of the Trinity" is a "crude" one, or with the ruling out of the "Atone- ment." Of course, if you do rule out the for- mer, the latter goes with it. There may be,' and no doubt have been and are, crude inter- pretations of both doctrines, but the truths themselves are accepted to-day by the Church Catholic, as they have been for many cen- turies. It, the Church Catholic, believes that prayer, as understood to-daY, had its birth in the Heart of the Triune Deity, and necessarily so. It believes that Redemption by Jesus, and Regeneration by the Holy Spirit, as well as the very existence of the God revealed by Jesus, is made possible by the stupendous fact of the Trinity, at Once the key-stone of Revela- tion, and its culminating point. • Notwithstanding all this, it seems to me dis- astrouS to ostracise churches and clergy because of the name they bear. Their position ought to be 'more clearly defined, and at • shorter inter- vals than it is, and the Church Catholic ought to take the trouble to find out which, not being against her, is for her, and for her Lord. There is undoubtedly a type of Unitarianism between which and the Trini- tarian Creed there is no practical difference. Starting from the opposite points, they -meet at a common goal. This is seen in the. former lifting the Blessed Lord, a man, up to God; while the latter sees in Him God come down to man. Where is the true difference? I think the Apostolic Benediction belongs to both. It will matter less in the Day of Judgment what theories we have held- about God, but much more will it matter if the Vitality and Grace, stood for in our theories by Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, actually dwell in our hearts and shine in our lives. Christianity may possess, hut is itself not a creed. Its orthodoxy is love. Its heresy is the lack of it.

W. REID.

Trinity Presbyterian Church, Canonbury, London, N. September 13th, 1916. •

===== 0002601_19160916_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KAISER'S ASPIRATION AND ITS FULFILMENT..

Sir,--At the present stage it would pernaps be as well to take a survey of what may be con- sidered to be the state of the war area in Europe at the close of hostilities, whenever that may be. , Viewed from,the German standpoint, it was a magnificent idea to aspire to an Empire whose western frontier should reach the North Sea and whose eastern boundary should ' extend to Persia and the borders of India, and that that Empire should have fortified bases and com- mercial ports abutting on the North Sea, the Adriatic, and IE gean Seas, with the alternative route of a railway line from Berlin. through Vienna to Constantinople, with control of the Straits, with a further railway line from the Asiatic coast of the Straits, and a branch from Smyrna, through Bagdad, to the Persian Gulf, having a fortified base and commercial port in the Gulf, and a branch from this line to the f Afghanistan. Surely this was a mag- nificent dream, but with all the forty years of German military training and Germany's com- mercial underhand dealing, it remains to-day an unfulfilled aspiration in the Kaiser's brain, growing more distant from view each day. Turning from the Kaiser's dream of aggran- disement to the realities of life, we come nack again to the devastation of Europe, and English- men may be proud of what they have done on land and sea, and even in the air, to stay the tyrant's power. Britain, had she so preferred, might have maintained her rights by her Navy alone, without putting a soldier in the field, but

===== 0002601_19160916_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====

make them at home. Cross-stitch, blanket-stitch, smocking, and beads, .are all used to decorate the schoolgirl's blouses Several adjustable sets ,of collars and cuffs give economical variety to quite plain blouses, and should be included in the autumn outfit. There are numerous adap- tations .of the jumper dress, for the girl in her early teens, and the idea ads excellent, as one jumper can be worn with half a dozen different blouses. Skirts may have a high, or the normal waist, and \be yoked, pleated, or plain. The pleated skirt gives height, and should be chosen for the short, stout girl, Coatfrocks that are as indispensable to. the girl as to her elders, have pleated or gored skirts, as preferred. The *litt,sian blouse in many variations, and the yoked Norfolk coat, are both suitable and in .favour this autumn. , A very charming model school dress for a girl of fourteen was made of blue" serge with a sailor blouse. It had a yoked skirt, but the yoke showed neither in front nor behind, for it was covered by a wide pleat that reached from the hem to the waist, a narrow pleat on either side of the wide one was put into, the yoke, and the sides of the skirt. were gored. Patch pockets were placed on. the yoke.

The Vogue for Black. With laundry charges soaring like everything else, the housewife. is constantly devising economies that shall lessen the weekly bill, and one of these is the dressing-table set of black net, and black for some unknown wagon is fashionable just now for cushions anti table decorations. When relieved by flowers appliqued in bright colours, these sets are decidedly pretty, and they last a long time, as the dust can be shaken off the net very easily. The penchant for black is seen also in the lucky handkerchiefs with a black cat embroidered in one corner, and in the initial wristlet, a favourite parting gift, consisting of the giver's initial S in diamonds fastened to a black watered ribbon. Black pottery is having quite a vogue for the table and flower vases, &c. Regimental badges ar6 donned by numbers of women, and quite a new idea is to enclose them in crystal lockets or brooches, ' and so to arrange them; that they appear to be: painted on the inner layer of the locket. Charms .in gold are also engraved with the badge. ‘,

Vegetable Marrow. • ---- Clean and cut the marrow into pieces and boil in salted water till tender, 'but yet firm. Do not boil till Ml is cooked, but remove each piece as it is done. Drain the pieces in a cloth. Then place them on a dish, season' lightly with salt, and cover with a thickly buttered (or substitute) paper. Place in the oven till the water is evaporated, and the butter absorbed by the vegetable. When marrow is served in this manner it is more appetising than the usually watery mass with white sauce thrown over.

Swede Turnips. Swede turnips may be attractively served alone, or on buttered toast with poached egg, or even with a little bread saute if carefully cooked and 'mashed. Peel the turnips, and after cut- ting them into pieces of equal size, let them remain for a short time in cold water. Then put them into boiling • water, and as soon as they have come to boiling point "gain add a little• salt and cook till' tender. The time for this will vary According to the age of the tur- nips. Squeeze "theta as dry as possible, pressing a saucer firmly on the top of them as they drain; then beat them well with a fork, and add some fat—butter, margarine, or dripping—and a little salt and pepper.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Openings for the Middle-aged Woman. It has been stated officially that there is a real and urgent need for more nurses in the military and other hospitals at home, at the Front, and in the Voluntary Aid Detachments, also of general, service members for that organisation. Women of middle-age are no longer a drug in the labour market, for their aid is sought in Government and other offices, and if intelligent, fairly good at figures, and good writers, no training for the kind of clerical work waitino to be done is necessary. Canteens for munition workers, more especially for those who work at night, are calling aloud for voluntary helpers, whilst from agriculturists all over the country comes the demand for more and still more women to help with the harvests.

The British Ambulance Committee. The British Ambulance Committee, which has worked Wonders in France for the past two years, owed its inspiration to- Mr. Bradley Peyman, who, knowing the conditions of the motor industry in France, and foreseeing a shortage of 'cars for the conveyance of the wounded to the hospitals, formed' an influential committee, and in a very short time a service of ambulances and, men was at work along the French lines. The workers who daily risk their lives have well-earned the twenty-nine Croix de Guerre, and the Medaille Militaire awarded certain members by the French Government. There are at the present moment two hundred and fifty men working one hundred and fifty ambulances, including the convoy lately presented by the personnel of Lloyds. A; convoy consists of twenty ambu- lances, which travel very swiftly with their wounded, although the roads are- rough, and often damaged, because the Germans delight in dropping bombs on. Red Cross motors. Mrs. Cecil Baker is the indefatigable honorary secretary to the committee. Magpie Effects.

Variety in checks and crossbars has been achieved by weaving the pattern diagonally, and some quite unusual effects have been thus attained. Diagonal crossbars in black and white are very popular just now, in fact the vogue for magpie effects has led manufacturers to specialise in this direction with the happiest results. Some of the black flowers on a dead white ground look as if they had been stencilled rather than printed. Stripes are again used horizontally and diagonally on hems to contrast with the vertical stripe of the upper part of the dress. Frequently stripes of different widths achieve a novel effect. Jet is very much worn with black and white toilettes, even by women who are not in mourning, it is also seen .on delicate pale shades of blue and pink, and it gives a touch of smartness otherwise difficult of attaThinent. Necklaces and chains of jet can be made from the beads on old-fashioned trim- mings and from old jetted-lace anyone possess- ing time and patience and some ingenuity, can ecoomise in this direction and attain smartness at small cost. The Autumn School Outfit.

The schoolgirl cannot have too many blouses in reason, and these can now be bought so cheaply that it is Waste of time and money to

she chose the better part, and joined her friends in the struggle for freedom, liberty and righteousness. Her honour and name were great before the war; they will be considerably greater at its close. Britain may, in the first place, be thankful to that All-Wise Creator, who has sur- rounded these little -islands with the silver streak, which enables her to be the home of liberty; second, to the power of her Navy; and last, but not least, of the willingness of her sons to serve her cause and risk life and limb on her behalf.

In contrast to our own dear and happy land, which has been saved from the invader's foot, except what he may have been able to do from the air, we Tnay turn to those regions of devas- tated Europe, where the enemy has been able to work his sweet will. He has wrought his will to an absolute extent over all the territory he ha l; invaded without remorse and without pity; nothing has been sacred from his unholy grasp, nothing has softened his heart or escaped his cupidity; the rich have been robbed and plundered and the poor have fared no better; fields have been stripped of their ,produce, mills and works of their machinery; villages, towns, and cities have had levies laid upon them in money or kind, until they are as poor as crows; even mines and other similar properties have been worked for the benefit of the enemy. Where flourishing towns and villages once stood it is now a howling wilderness, and many landmarks that record some of the noblest events, of his- tory are destroyed. For what are these things done? Simply that the greatest robber chief and plunderer of all time should have his will and the greatest pandemonium in history be enacted by the nation which puts for- ward the claim to be the most enlightened and cultured of the present age. This is what it calls "spreading kultur." These people have by their preparedness and their sudden onslaught at the beginning of hos- tilities been enabled to carry their depredations into territory that was not theirs and now comes the necessity to drive them out. In so doing it is beyond a doubt that the Allies will, be com- pelled to complete the destruction the Germans have not quite tfinished ; and while we consider this point, let us be thankful that we are out- side the active warzone. It Inust be an absolute understood decision that Germany at the close of the war shall restore all the parts of Europe to their former state, as a consequence of having failed in the task it undertook to perform, and should not only do so, but compensate the in- habitants thereof for their mental and bodily suffering. This should be absolute, and German territory should be occupied and held until the task is completed; this being independent of ally war indemnity the Allies may chose to demand from the enemy for their, expenditure of blood and Nothing will compensate for the lives lost, and those who are the authors of this terrible devastation will go to their doom with a heavy weight of blood guiltiness upon their shoulders to meet that offended Majesty the King of kings, Whom they so airily professed to serve: What a commentary this war is on forty years of scientific learning and advance- ment in this advanced age of humanity and Christian teaching! PHIL SQUOD.

LEITCH—BA in't% IN.

Khaki Wedding At Warrington Parish

-Church.

A weking which aroused considerable local interest was solemnised at the Warrington Parish- Ghurch on Thursday between Charlotte Baldwin, daughter of James Baiawin, 22, Pad- gate-lane, and Private James Leitch (son of Mr. and Mrs. F. J. Leitch, 63, Padgate-lane), who is at present serving in the A.S.C., at- tached to the West Lancs. -Division, now train- ing in the South of England. The bride, who was neatly attired in a grey, costume, was given away by her father. Her niece, Miss Mgdge Baldwin, acted as bridesmaid. After the wed- ding breakfast, which was held at Finger Post Farm, the home of the bride's brother, Mr. Thomas ,§ Baldwin, the happy couple left for Blackpool, where the honeymoon is bent spent.

£2,000 DAMAGE.

Curtain Of Water Prevents Fire

Spreading.

That greatly more than ...t2,000 worth of damage was not done at a fire on the riverside on Monday night was solely due to the enter- prise of the Fire Brigade, under Supt. Frank Turner.

While on point duty at Bridge Foot, Special Constable Richardson suddenly observed a flare above the saw mills belonging to Mr. James H. Hulme, and; discovering that a fire had broken out, he rushed across to the Academy, where he has spent many rePoseful hours, ,and telephoned to the Fire Station. "All orders promptly executed" is the maxim of the Fire Brigade, and, happily, there being seven men on the premises the motor engine was away with them and on the scene in "no time," as one spectator remarked. Very quickly the hydrants were got to work, and the Superintendent, noting the inflammable material with which he had to deal, and the congested area of the district—several works and cottage property standing contiguous with Mt Hulme's mills—sent for the steam fire engine, which duly arrived, the large crowd, which bad by now assembled, making way for the panting horses, heralded by the loudlr clang- ing bell. By this time the flames could be seen all over the district, the sky being illumined brightly over an extensive area. It was a great attraction, and the eager spectators, had to be kept at a safe distance by a posse of regular police, assisted by special constables, under the ' supervision of Chief Constable Nicholls. • As the minutes sped the adjoining works and warehouse on each side of the saw mills looked doomed, the following being in immediate peril —the plumber's stores of Messrs. T. Allen and Son, the joiner's shop of Mr. Samuel Norbury, the warehouse of the Bishop's Wharf Carrying Company, the tool works of Messrs. Thewlis and Griffiths, Ltd., and the hay and coin stores of Mr. T. M. West. Sparks and pieces of burning timber were carried by the wind in the direction of the Castle Rubber Co., Ltd., and to protect that works all the windows were closed and the work's brigade stood by with extinguishing ap- pliances in case. of need. Workmen in the other places mentioned prepared for any development of, the ravaging flames. Outbreak Rounded Up.

It can be seen that the Fire Brigade a difficult task before them. The dispositions of the hose itself required ingenuity, but very soon ten powerful jets from both engines and hydrants were being trained on to the fire, some of the hose having to be carried over the roofs of the adjoining Works. Always the risk to the latter was at a premium, and in order to pro- tect them a curtain of water was directed be- tween them and the edges of the fire. In an hour and a half the Brigade had the satisfaction of observing that the outbreak was under con- trol. The motor engine and a number of assistants were able to return to headquarters about midnight, the other engine and the re- mainder of the men staying eight hours longer. Result of the fire: IMr. Hulme's saw mills completely gutted, Mes§rs. Allen and Son's steres and "premises partly destroyed, though, happily, the paint was untouched, and slight damage by water to -the premises of Messrs. Thewlis and Griffiths and the Bishop's Wharf Carrying'

Company. Damage about 0,000. The origin of the fire is wrapt in mystery. The 'bulk of the property and contents is in- sured.

DRUNKENNESS AMONG WASPS.

A correspondent writes: While on a black- berrying expedition in a not rediote but un- frequented part of Surrey, I came across a spot where,' apparently, the regulations of the Liqporl Control 13(wd are unknown or ignored. It was during prohibited hours and there was much , drinking and drunkenness going on— among wasps. . It was in a little copse of low brushwood and brambles where the berries had grown over-ripe. The air reeked with the smell of blackberry juice, and the wasps were so busy and so tipsy that they hardly resented interference. They seemed to be in the maudlin or affectionate stage, and when driven from one cluster simply rolled on to another. lam not a naturalist and wonder whether the wasp is accustomed to debase itself in this way.

ADVANTAGES OF A GOOD EDUCATION.

My Dear Children,— I have had many excellent essays on the advantages of a goad education and, as is my custom, I reproduce below some' of the letters and extracts from others so that you may see what your different cousins think. You will see that all agree that there are real advan- tages in becoming as good scholars as you pos- sibly can. It is to be hoped that you will act up to this belief, because we want our English boys and girls to beat German boys and girls when they become men and women. I think there is no doubt on That point, but we must not miss any chance through believing we are sure to win. The prize this week goes to 1002 Niece Dorothy Lowery, 39, Leicester-street, age 13, who attends Evelyn- street School. Evelyn-street School claimed the prize winner last week, and must have a good supply of clever children. This week we will have an essay on

A Town In France I Would Like To Visit,

giving the reasons why.

Uncle Ned will be glad to know that several of his nieces and nephews have written to say that they are pleased he has been on fuflough, and to know that he is "all right." Uncle Ned is "all right" in more ways than one, and no one wishes more for; his return than Your loving, UNCLE JACK.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRIZE ESSAY.

From Niece Dorothy Lowery, 39, Leicester- street, No. 1,002 (Evelyn-street School). "Dear Uncle Jack,—l think that the com- position on,' The Advantages of a Good Educa- tion' is a very nice subject. I think that a is a part of parents' duty to give their children a good education. The people who do not are ruining the children's later life, besides not doing their duty. The first and greatest advan- tage of a good education is that it enables people to obtain a good position in life. If when they are young they go to school regu- :arly and work hard they will no doubt be in the top standard when they leave school, and would be able to take up a better position than the person who reaches no higher than Standard V. Another advantage is that it enables people to get a good salary. If people had a good education they would perhaps be able to get a situation in an office .when they leave school, and they would therefore have a better salary than the per,son who has to _d., hard, manual labour. People who are well ,educated have the pleasure of being able to read and write, besides knowing all about geography, history, literature, and many other useful lessons. Every English boy and girl has the chance of having a good education, for in every town schools are erected for the pur- pose of educating children. Every father and mother should make an effort to give their chil- dren a better education than they have had, or the rising generation will no be able to reach to the top of the ladder."

===== 0002601_19160916_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER OPINIONS.

I have had a letter from a little niece who.. is only seven years of age. She does not tell me of the advantages of a good education, but she lets me know how beginning to learn has bene- fited her. ' She writes:—

"8, Monks-street, Warrington, September 12th, 1916. "Dear Uncle Jack,—Before I went to school I could not read nor write, but now I can do both. I have just gone to the big 'school, and I am learning to do sums.—Your loving niece, Dora Edwards, No. 999."

Dora is at the doorway that leads to a good education—especially now she is learning to dr) sums at such a 7 good school as Arpley-street School.

Niece Lily Will iamson, .\53, Priestley-street (Evelyn-street School) :—" A good education has something to do with our work when we leave school. For instance; at school we are taught domestic science, and if a girt who has not been to school goes as a domestic servant she will not know how to do anything. We are taught sums, and if a girl who has not been to school goei. in a shop to serve she cannot reckon. It has been mentioned in the paper that children ought to go to school until fifteen years of age. I think some ought, for there are children in the lowest classes when ready to leave'. There are private schools where, children have to pay,

but what is the use of paying when- we can go free? If everybody went to school at a proper time and finished in a decent standard there would not be very ninny children unfit for work."

Niece Fanny Osborne, 31, Leicester-street (Evelyn-street School) :—" Education is nedes- sary to civilisation. Our missionaries going abroad and teaching the people in other lands have brought about the great extension of our Colonies. Our English merchants have taught the natives the value of trade. Germany must have been well educated to be able to-carry on such a great war; but I think we can go one better than they, for our great men have been able to play them at their own game, and in the end we shall be able to teach them some- thing."

Niece Mabel Webb, 69, Oldham-street (Bolton Council School) :—" A girl or boy who is going to be put to clerical work would get a better situation if they could give the name of a college they had attended, because they would be more fit in grammar, and therefore would put better letters together. A boy who had been given a good education would be more fit for a traveller, simply because he could better explain about the different goods. Children who have not been well educated could not address a person with a title without being abashed and perhaps blundering a little. Therefore it is an advantage for all people to have a good educa- tion, whether in a high or low station." Nephew Frank Edwards, 8, Monks-street:— "One of the greate.st. advantages is that a boy who is well educated has a better chance of having a good occupation than eboy who is not so well eclicated. A good education makes a boy or girl have good manners; teaches them to be polite, and enables them to build up a good character."

Niece Edna Fe110w5,,44, Bewsey-road (Evelyn- street School):—" Besides helping us in our daily work, a good education is a help in domestic affairs. If we could not count, read or write, girls would not be alple to become good housekeepers. Our bills would be a constant trouble to us, and we would be Xii a very poor plight indeed. I am glad to say there are not many schools- where the children have to pay, and also a much better education is obtain- able. I think that we (Jug,ht to be very grate- ful, and that we ought to learn as much as we possibly can."

Nephew Frank Overend, 74, Lythgoes-lane i (Hamilton-street School), says it s important that we should put energy into our work and that study at night school should follow study at day school. Nephew T. Roberts. 6, Portland-street (Silver- street School), says that all the world has bene- fited from -the.work of its great rden, who have been educated from early life. Without educated men such as Shakespeare, Gladstone, Milton and others we should not have been so advanced as we are. Some of our greatest statesmen were well taught; it is easier now for boys to be taught, for they have every chance to learn if they desire to do so.

s Niece Maud A Lunny, 108 a, Bridge-street *ttArpley-street School):—" It is good for chil- dren to be instructed properly, as it strengthens the mind and body, and it will be a credit all through life if they are patient and learned. Many persons are sorry for not doing as they were told, in younger days, and so are bad scholars."

Niece Lily McLachlan, 8, Ash-grove, Latch- ford (Bolton Council School) :—" To have a good education means to have knowledge to understand things. One advantage is that you can get a good situation in an office when you leave school, and earn good money. A. good education is a good advantage to most boys if they want to, learn a trade. The German boys and girls are trying to beat us in education, but they have not succeeded in doing so yet."

Nephew John Webb, /27, Catherine-street :- "Some boys and girls, although they do not like school, can see now, why they should go. Most of the duffers leave as soon as they can, but they soon find that their opinion of school is wrong when they look for a place and are questioned. Boys who go in for office boys must be able to write quickly, do a large sum in a very few minutes and know how much the workmen have to be paid if he is in a works

office. They soon find where a 'good education' comes in."

Nephew Harry Heywood, 8, Matthews-streat (Parochial) :—" All the Generals in our Army and Captains and Admirals in our Navy are well educated, which is the best thing for us all. Men like Lord Kitchener got their posi- tions through one thing, which is good educa- tion. England would not have been as great as she is now if there were no . schools to be educated at. Education has made England great and will make it greater in years to come." Niece Ethel Shaw, Sunnyside, Chester New. road (Bolton CouncirSchool):—" A good educa- tion may be obtained now at a very small .cost. Scholars can begin at an ordinary public school and if they attend well to their lessons they have the chance of obtaining a scholarship to go to the Secondary School or Grammar School; after this, if they again take an interest in their tessting_ they can go to a college or university."

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 16, 1916.

A WONDERFUL WEEK t OF WAR.

The Germans • digged a pit before Verdun for the French, and they have suffered them- selves in the consequent landslide on the banks of the Somme. With reckless prodi- gality, both id life and material, the enemy for months pounded the defences of _Verdun: That prodigality might have justified itself had the defending garrisons been less heroic than our brave French Allies, but the latter stood firm, and, on ground which Mr. LLOYD GEORGE says, and says truly, must ever be regarded as holy ground, they saved humanity.. After months of bleeding at an: unprecedented rate, Germany was iklueed to hoping that at least France had been bled, too, and upon the same lavish scale. But France came, up smiling on the Somme, and, along with our own Forces, have wrested the offensive from the hands of the foe. Our com- bined efforts are gaining. on the enemy, ,and gaining at an increasing rate. Each day this week has brdught its victory, so that we have at every step been constrained to think that a rest must be taken if only for a breathing space., But last night's news was more ;glorious than all that' had preceded it. We advanced over a six miles front to a' depth, in 'some places, of 3,000 yards, and the French, who had already got astride the German com- munications between Peronne and Bapaume, went still further ahead. It looks uncom- monly like having got the ,Hun really on the run at last. He would doubtless give a good deal pow for the resources he threw away in his, half a year's fruitless efforts at Verdun. Nor is this the whole of the picture. Whi.e we are engaging the attention of the enemy Russia is coming along, tile zillies are ni-vin;i: in Greece, where even the gallant Serbs arc. again finding their feet and trouncing the Bulgarians, Roumania is Making good at a tremendous rate iii Transylvania, and the Italians are engaging the attention of the Austrians to very good purpose. Tn brief, the enemy are dancing to our tune in all quar- ters of this widest flung battlefield of all ages.

We have assumed the general offensive; he has lost it. This satisfactory stage ha viaz been reached, he has definitely lost the war and we have won. All we have to do is to " stick it," a condition which the KAISER knows, too well, that we shall fulfil.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
GOSSIP.

The song running through everyone's mind, last week was "Tennessee," 'sung by Miss Beatrice Allen, at the Royal Court Theatre. It is curious how it maintained its popularity.

Even on Anday, while passing along Lovely- lane,. I heard two young ladies singing it, pre- sumably, as part of their morning devotions. Turning into Wellfield-street the hymn, "For- give Them Father, 0 ForgiCe,"'was being sung with the deepest (it was a bass voice) sincerity.

"Liverpool, near • Warrington," say old topographical books. On Monday the "Liver- pool Echo" seemed in ecstacies because their first lady taxi-cab driver had, arrived. We have had ladies in Warrington acting in this capacity for several months. Warrington again takes precedence of Liverpool.

The many admirers of his talent will be in. terested to know that Mr. Bradford Barton, the violinist, has just been discharged from the Army suffering from rheumatism and a dis- placement of a bone in his foot.

Mr. Chas. Dukes having been consigned to the Army, Mr. A. E. Seabury has been appointed ,secretary pro tem. of the Trades -and Labour Council. , They are birds—rare birds—of a feather. 'But Mr. Dukes is only a member of the executive of the British Socialist Party (anti-war section): Mr. Seabury is chairman.

A Tribunal sitting at the Town Hall is not the dull place it might be taken for, judging from the meagre attendance of the public.

On Thursday an applicant was backing up his case by stating that his company slipplied coal to munition works. "You also supply coal to the Town Hall," interrupted Mr. Hallaway, the clerk, "but this is not a munition works— although we have some big guns here," he added, inclining his eyes to the Tribnhal mem- bers.

"And high explosives at times." added Captaip Mathams. The news fell like a bombshell among the Tribunal.

A ,Tribunal case was dispdsed of in record time at the Towni Hall yesterday. Applicant (answering to his name): Since putting in my appeal I have been badged. Mr. Ha'lawny (clerk): Thank you, good morning. Exit.

VIGILANT.

ODDFELLOWSH IP.

"Olive Branch " Lodge, AVarrinitton

District, M.U.

On Saturday evening a pleasant time was spent at the above Lodge, the occasion being a presentation of a framed Past Officer's Certifi- cate to Past Grand John Alexander Brown. The district officers had been invited and had promised to attend, but,. unfortunately, the District Grand Master (Bro. W. E. Clare) and his deputy, Bro. Owen Ashton, were prevented from putting in an appearance, and in their absence P.P.G.M. Frank Webster (the district secretary), kindly consented to make the pre- sentation.

The officers of the Lodge i attendance were N.G. W. Taft (presiding), V.G, ElleStuore, Lodge G.M. 'Richard 'Whitfield, Minute Secre- tary Stanley . Whitfield, P.G. Arthur, Cotterill (treasurer). and P.P.G.M. Alban Boyle (secre- tary).. and the visiting brethren included P.P.G.M. William Holbrook, P.P.G.M. Ellis R. Clare and P.P.G.M. Walter Knowles. Presentation to P.G. J. A. Brown,

The District Secretary, in making the Pre- sentation, said it afforded him great pleasure to be the medium of conveying the thanks and appreciation of the "Olive Branch" ,Lodge to P.G. J. A. Brown for the able services rendered He understood that Bro. Brown had been called u for military service. In these strenuous Hines a young man never knew Vhen be would be ordered/to respond to a similar call. He (the speaker) felt sure that Bro. Brown would do his share for King and country, and that every one wished him a speedy and safe return. (Applause.) Lodge G.M. Whitfield congratulated Bro. Brown on qualifying for a P.O. certificate, and hoped it w'ould be an incentive to the younger members of the Lodge to come forward and take more interest in the work of the Lodge. P.P.G.M. Clare, P.P.G.M. Holbrook and P.G. John Brown also spoke in encouraging terms, exhorting the younger section of the Lodge to put forth every effort to mak-?, the Lodge a continued success. P.G. John A. Brdwn, in acknowledging the presentation, said he was pleaded that the Lodge had seen fit to recognise his services in such a substantial manner.

The meeting was pleasantly concluded by the singing of the hymn for absent brethren, with special reference to members of the Order serving with His Majesty's Naval and Military Forces at the war.

THE EXANIINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 16, 1916. DR. JOSEPH ADAMS. MR. JOSEPH DOLAN. I PROMOTIONS AND DISTINCTIONS.

Dies With Painful Suddenness Death Of Well-known Catholic At Blackpool. And Builder.

Many in Warrington and district were dis- tressed to hear of the death of Mr. Joseph Dolan, at his residence, Virestbourne, Belvoir- Iliad, Lower Walton, on Saturday night; in fact, the news came as a great shoek,for during the week he had moved about in his usual way, 'although for two or three years he had been in a delicate state of health. Apparently much better for a stay of three months in Llandudno, of which he was par- ticularly fond, Mr. Dolan returned to his home on the 31st of last month. He was about to leave the house on Saturday morning to make a journey into town, when he was taken ill. Later in the day he took to his bed, at five o'clock he became unconscious, and, without rallying, passed peacefully away halt an hour btifore mid- night, Dr. Binns certifying death to be due to cerebral hemorrhage. For many years Mr. Dolan was prominent in the business life of Warrington. In 1871, when he was 22 Years of age, he founded a building business along with Mr. Toft,. carrying this on himself from 1871 to 1908, when his son became a partner, and he worked less strenuously, tak- ing we/11-earned periods of rest. A native of Warrington, he always took a deep, though for a gentleman of his social standing, an inconspicuous' interest in its wel- fare. A devout Roman Catholic, 'he was a mem- ber of the choir at St. Mary's (Buttermarket- street) for 25 years, frequently taking tenor solos. He was •senior trustee of the Catholic Philanthropic Society. By one who knew him for the greater part of his life it has been said that Mr. Dolan was "one to know whom was to respect, and to love —a kind, gentle and lovable character." In his business, too, he was regarded as a courteous and fair-dealing employer. A most honourable gentleman. In his honour the flags of all the Roman Catholic Churches in- the town flew at half- mast from the announcement of his death until his funeral.

A DOUBLE BEREAVEMENT.

Having only so short a time ago as Tuesday week buried his wife, Dr. Joseph Adams him- self passed away after a tragically short illness yesterday. Subsequent to his wife's death, the doctor was advised to,take a brief rest, and for that purpose motored to Blackpool yesterday week. He was accompanied by his daughters, Mrs. Jackson, commandant of the hospital for wounded soldiers at The Towers, Rainhill, an.l Miss Gladys Adams. That a fatal illness or an illness of any kind was approaching was not at all apparent, and even as late as Tuesday he wrote from the Hotel getropole: "This place is doing us all good, and to some extent distracts our sense of our great los. lam

===== 0002601_19160916_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
THE 'LATE DR. ADAMS

glad I came, as I feel I could not have gone straight on with work at home." • Soon after the arrival of this letter in War- rington on Wednesday morning a telegram was received stating that the doctor had been taken seriously ill. Ik. Findlay, of Blackpool, was immediately call Mi in, and subsequently a specialist, Dr. Warrington, of 'Liverpool, was brought into consultation. On Thursday, Dr. Langdale, an intimate friend of Dr. Adams, and who atfenaed Mrs. Adams in her last ill- ness, went to see him. Despite all medical care, the doctor died as stated.

We are sure that this announcement will he read with the greatest regret; for Dr. Adams was a well-known figure in the -town and dis- trict, not only. in his professional capacity, but also as an English gentlenfan. His was a striking personality—able and possessed of great medical skill and a vast store of general know- ledge. He had a wonderful capacity for work. which .enabled Min to 'fulfil his manifold duties with thoroughness and distinction. - Besides having one of the largest practices in the town of Warrington, he was Medical Officer of Health for the Runcorn -Rural District. He was • also a Company Commander of the Lancashire Volunteer Brigade and an Hon. Associate of the Order of St. John of Jerusalem in England. He was an eloquent and lticid lecturer on scientific subjects and ambulance work, and it may he ventured that he was greatly responsible for the efficiency of most of the ambulance men in the district.

Born in 1856 at Hanley, Staffordshire, he was educated at Northwood Academy, and spent two valuable years in the laboratories of the world- famous chemists—Duncan. Flockhart and Co., Edinburgh. He had a distinguished university career, in that city, and was especially proud of winning, in the same session, Profession -Huxley's senior medal in coraparative anatomy, and Professor Balfour's senior medal ip botany. He took his degree of Bachelor of Medicine and Master of Surgery in 1880. After professional experiences in Birmingham,, the Five Towns. Maryport and other places, he came to Warrington in .1883. In that year he was aPpointed Medical Officer to the Pearson and Knowles' Provident and Accident Society, and held that Position for 26 years. In 1884 he was elected Medical Officer of Health to the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority, now the Runcorn Rural District Council.• For many years he had been interested in the Volunteer movement, and on the mobilisa- tion of the Territorials in 'August, 1914, went with them to their training quarters as Surgeon-Mn jar. however, prevented his continuing longer than six or seven months and much to his regret. he was obliged to give UP all hope of going to the Front.' The doctor 'leaves. two daughters, who .are mentioned above, and a son, Mr. Harvey Adams, who is an actor, to mourn his, loss.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
Floral Exhibition at the Town Hall.

On Friday afternoon next Mrs. Rylands will open an eight days' Floral Exhibition and sale of artificial flowers at the Co-Operative Hall, Cairo-street, Warrington. The Mayor (Alder- man Peter Peacock) will preside. The exhibi- tion is organised by the Watercress and Flower Girls' Christian Mission, which is the only flower girls' institution in existence and was inaugurated in 1886 by the seventh Earl of Shaftesbury. There are 300 blind, crippled and afflicted girls in the schools and homes-of the Mission, drawn from all parts 'of .the United Kingdom. The girls are received without pay- ment or votes as resident pupils for two years, and are instructed in the making of artificial flowers for decorative and artistic purposes. In many, cases these flowers are not to be told from real blooms. After the two years' term the girls may, if they desire, return to their own homes with a remunerative calling at their fingers' ends. It ,was in 1879 that the flower- making branch was begun, to provide suitable remunerative employment for, girls who were unable to enter domestic service owing to phy- sical affliction. The general funds support an Orphanage of 300 girls, Convalescent Home, Cottage Hospital and other branches of the institution, and the suppori of the benevolent is needed for the upkeep and extension of this work.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
Father Maesson's Departure.

A very enjoyable smoking concert was held in St. Mary's Hall on Friday dvening last, when about eighty members or the C.Y.M.S. met and took farewell of their much-respected Chaplain, the Rev. Father Mawson, who is 'leaving Warrington during the coming week to take up important duties at Ampleforth College. The meeting was presided over by Father Hinde, rector of St. Mary's, who was supported by Father Willson, Father Dawson, Father Pears, and Father Chamberlain. 'Father .Hinde, in his own name and in the name of the parish, thanked Father Mawson for the manner in which he had performed' his many duties, and also for the assistance he had rendered to him as Rector. Bro. T. Dolan, V.P., thanked Father Mawson for the many favours he had shown to the socieV, and, in the name of the mem- bers, wished him God-speed. Bro. T. Naugh-, ton, St. Mary's, Widnes, on behalf of the War- rington and District Council, thanked Father Maw Son for the assistance he had given .to them. Father Mawson suitably responded and thanked the members for the assistance they had given to him during the seven years that .he had been their Chaplain. He appealed to them to continue their work as in the past. The concert was Continued until 11.30 p.m., when it, was brought .to a close by singing "Faith of our Fathers." The artistes were drawn from Widnes, Ditton, and the various local branches of the C.Y.M.S.

CLEVER SCHOLARS.

Brilliant Record Of Technical Institute Student.

Mr. Thomas Malkin, a student of the War- rington Technical Institute, who resides at the Royal Exchange Inn, Golden-square, and has been engaged by Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, Limited, for the past three years as laboratory assistant, has been awarded• the "Sir Henry Roscoe" Exhibition of £lO per annum for two years. This exhibition is ftwarded upon the results of the examinations of the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes in the Chemical Trades Course (sth year), 1916. The subjects of examination are: Inbrganic chemistry, organic ' chemistry, physical chemistry.

He has had quite a remarkable run of suc- cesses. He isxeighteen years of age. When fourteen he left Heathside Elementary Day School and became for a year laboratory assistant at the Secondary School and Techni- cal Institute. He • has taken the chemical courses at the latter Institute for the last four seasqns, gaining ,a scholarship there four years ago.. • _

-11i's record of successes is as follows : First year.—Magnetism and -electricity, second class certificate; chemistry, first class mathematics, first class, with distinction. Second year.—Mathematics, first class certifi- cate.

Third year.—Organic chemistry, first class certificate. Gained Union of Lancashire and Cheshire prize of a chemical laboratory. Fourth year.—First place in Lancashire and Cheshire in fifth year chemical course examina- tion; and the Roscoe exhibition mentioned/ above.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
To Aid Stricken Ally Farmers.

Katharine Duchess of Westminster, the Duchess of Teck. Princess Mary of Teck, and Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall were among the auctioneers at an exhibition and sale at Chester on Thursday on behalf of the Agricul- tural Relief (Allies') Fund. Katharine Duchess of Westminster opened the sale with a shorthorn calf given by the Duke of Westminster, which was twice sold to Sir Gilbert Preenall and finally realised £177. The Duche4s of Teck sold a shire filly,/ also given by the Duke of West- minster, for 70 guineas, and Princess Mary sold a _Shropshire lamb ram for £39. A famous donkey which has figured at many of these sales made over £lOO. bringing its total for patriotic purposes to £l,lOO.

CAPTAIN W. S. FURNESS.

Promotion In The Field For An Old

Secondary School Roy.

Promotion in the Field.—The following appears on the Divisional Orears : " Lieut. W. S. Furness, Royal Enniskilling Fusiliers, to be captain, and to command a company, with effect from July 3, 1915."

The above notice recently posted with refer- ence to Lieut. W. S. Furness, son of Mr. W. Furness, Fern Lea, Great Sankey, and a former scholar of People's College, student at the War- rington Secondary School, and teacher at Bea- mont School, reflects the official appreciation of valuable services rendered over a considerable period. Captain Furness joined the Colours as a private immediately on the ottbreak of war, and received his commission within a few weeks of joining. He had previously served in the Warrington Territorials (Transport section) for four years. gaining the effieiency prize in the last of those years: He also seised two years in his college corps in London when train- ing for a teneher. He has led ninny raiding parties upon the German trenches, both on the eve of the great advance and since, and was one

of the first to go .over the parapet when the for- ward move began on July 1." Fortunately he has not been seriously 'curt, though he was recently blown into a shell hole and suffered' slightly from shell Shock. His sergeant, who won the D:C:M. for the part he played in one of .the raids, is now in a Man- chester hospital wounded.

WHO IS "MAGGIE" P

Dying Soldier's Request To

Comrade.

PATHETIC SCENE.

His clothes sodden with rain, there tame into "The Examiner" Office, Bewsey-street, yesterday afternoon, a soldier who said he was looking for a girl. "Could you help me to find her? I've been looking for her for two days," he said. "It all. depends upon, the circum- stances," we replied. He then told a remark- able story.

First let it be stated that his name is Private William Booth Holden, of 33, Ellesmere-street, Howley. "Only 16th," he slid, "just before an attack, I was in the trenches and chummed along with a fellow named Gilbert, of a Liverpool Regiment. In the . attack we had just gone over the parapet, when Gilbert fell back wounded. I , went to him, and, struggling hard and• breathing heavily, as if death ~)vere upon him, he took out of his breast pocket a photograph Awhich we reproduce) and panted Give that to Maggie. She lives in Howley.' That was all he could say. I thought he said 'Maggie,' but am not certain.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
" MAGGIE."

He then fell back unconscious. I pocketed the photograph and went on into the charge. I was told that he died very shortly afterwards. "For two days now I've been hunting for the girl, but can't find her. In the cold. of yester- day and the rain of to-day I've been tramping all about Howley, showing the portrait to all kinds of people. NearV everyone seems to recognise her, but cannot say where she lives. That is why I've come to you." Such an appeal could not go undenied. Since Private Holden heard the dying re- quest of a comrade he has been in eight charges; being wounded last month. He has just been discharged from hospital, and one of his first duties has been that described. If the lady herself, or anyone who knows her, will communicate with him he will be grateful. Before enlisting Private Holden worked in the coal department at the Co-operative Stores.
