FRODSHAM SEII6EINrS SUCCESS.

Wins Championship In Bayonet Charge Competition.

It is very gratifying to hear of the success of sergeant Algernon C. B. Clark, son of Mr. and Mrs. Clarke, of Delph Cottage, Five Crosses. Quite / recently he had the opportunity of entering a great competition held somewhere in France, open only to C.O.'s and N.C.O.'s from Sergeants upwards. Picked men from all. regi- ments in the British Expeditionary Force took part in the competition. Sergeant Clarke con- tested against over 400 officers, and finally carried .off the championship in the bayonet charge, and with it the much-coveted silver medal. He was later congratulated by the General. His company are exceedingly proud of thp honour he has won, as he is greatly esteemed by ll his men. He has had many narrow escapes whilst doing night duty and directing the scouts; he is n„ost daring and fearless. His many friends will be delighted to hear of his success and safety, and wish him a safe return home to his parents in the near future.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
SERGEANT BRADBURY.

Tribunal Member's Son Severely . IVounded.

Mr. Bradbury, a member of the Warrington Tribunal, has received news that his sOa, Sergeant Bradbury, of the R.W.F., has been admitted to a French clearing station suffering from severe and serious abdominal wounds. "An operation was performed upon him," ran the message, "and for a while he did well. He has been going to write to you, but doesn't feel able to do so this morn,ing. I hope thisiis only a temporary set back, and that he will be better to-moriow. I shall write to you again, as I am sure you are very anxious. He sends his love to you all." Sergeant Bradbury has been at the Front • a long time, having spenttwo birthdays and eaten two Christmas dinners, in the trenches.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
Lady BOwlers.

There was a large entry for the ladies' bowl- ing main at the King and Queen Hotel on Thursday. The main was open to the wives of the members of the club. The weather was fine and the green in good condition, with the 'result that the ladies enjoyed themselves to the fullest extent. The President s:d the Club (Mr. T. S. Steel) and Mrs. Steel were present, and during the afternoon provided the com- petitors and their friends with tea. Many of the games were keenly contested, and the final and semi-final games were worthy of experi- enced male players. Each competitor was given a prize,' the first prize consisting of a gold bangle, which was won by Mrs. Starkey, and the second of a ease of fish knives and forks, won by Mrs. Lee. At the close of the main the prizes were distributed by 'Airs. Steel, and the President voiced the thanks of all present to Mr. and Mrs. F. Starkey- for the admirable arrangements they had made: A vote of thanks was accorded Mrs. Steel for distributing the prizes.:

PROMOTIONS AND DISTINCTIONS.-

Continued.

me th‘t you have distinguished yourself by your conduct in the field. I have read their report with much pleasure, and have brought it to the notice of higher authority." • The final message, dated 18th August, was sent from the headquarters of the Seventh Army Corps to Mrs. Neeve, who reside at 87, Baxter-street, 'Torrington. It ran as follows: "I heartily yongratulate you on the honour done you by His Majesty the King in awarding you the Military Medal for your gallant conduct on the First July, 1916." Rifleman Neeve Was wounded in.the hand on that date, and, being in hospital at Cambridge, did not know of his award until his wife wrote to him.,

Private Neeve, who is a native of Norwich, was called up on August 4th, 1914, having been married only three days before. He was soon drafted to the Front, where he was acting as stretcher-bearer. He was wounded in the arm in December, 1914, but was not brought to England. He was ' formerly employed by Messrs. Thorne and Co. in the building of War- rington Bridge, and has since resided in town. His brother Harry was lost on a mineskveeper last January, while his wife has got two brothers in the Army, Joseph with the .A.S.C. 'in France, and Wm. Frederick with the National Reserve in , the Isle ,of Man.

Harvest Festivals.

• To-morrow harvest festival services wil be held at- Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, Padgate Wesleyan Church, and begh-street Primitive Methodist Church. Next Thursday evening and Sunday similar services will be held. at St - Ann's Church.

Scarlet Fever Disappearing. During the week ended September 9th, the following new cases of infectious .disease in Warrington, were notified :—Measles, 8; tuber' tulosis, 3; diphtheria and scarlatina, one each - The following cases are now in hospital : Scarlet fever, 17; enteric fever, 1; diphtheria, 5; phthisis (Sankey Hospital), 13; total, 36.

Crosfield's Directors.

The directors of JOseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited, soapmakers, Warrington, have elected Messrs. Roscoe Brunner, J.P., Emile S. Mond. and Giles Hunt to seats on the Board. Mr; Roscoe Brunner has been elected chairman or the directors, in the place of the late Mr. V- W. Brock.

Births Many; Deaths Few. • During the week ended September 9th there were born in Warrington 32 children, eighteen males and fourteen females, against 38 in the corresponding week last year. Deaths nuin- bered only fourteen against 29 last year. The fourteen deaths resulted from: Bron- chitis, 2; diarrhoea, cancer, heart disease ati4 accident, one each; all other causes, 8.

The Pioneer Band will play the following selections of music in the Park to-morrow' (Sunday), at 3.15 :—Hymn, " Compstall (Wadsworth); quick march, "King Lear (Rimmer); selection, " Euryanthe " (Weber); overture, "La Ruche D.0.R." Blinded Soldiers and Sailors. . We again draw attention to the concert which is to be given in the Parr Hall next Frid.g evening, 22nd September, by "The Blind Musicians," in aid of the soldiers and sailors who have been blinded in the war. A hostel for the reception of these stricken heroes ,has been established at St. Dunstan s, Regent's Park, London, and it is hoped that the coricer will realise a substantial sum on its behalf.

The Star of Hope. An exceedingly attractive post card, printed in several colours, has just been issued by the Royal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals in connection with their fund for the relief of wounded horses at the front. SERGEANT J. O'NEILL.

Fell While Gallantly Leading

His Men.

Official Dews has been reeeivel that Sergeant J. O'Neill, familiarly kngwn as "Buff "O'Neill, was killed in action on the 18th August. Writing to the dead soldier's brother, Mr. William O'Neill, 59, Algernon-street, Captain G. Mather said :—" The regiment nad to attack and he was killed instantly by machine gun fire While gallantly -leading his men. He had absolutely no sense of fear, and was one of our best sergeants. He is a great loss to the com- pany. I am afraid his body was not brought in; search parties went out, hut his body was not found."

Sergeant O'Neill, 'Wlio was thirty-five years of age, had eight years' service to his credit. He fought in the latter stages of the South

KILLED.

CAPTAIN F. C. SINGLETON.

Promising Young Officer Falls

In First Engagement.

Captain Frank Chester Singleton, of the Sherwood Foresters, son Of the late Mr. James Singleton, headmaster of St. Peter's School, Newton-le-Willows, and of Mrs. Singleton, 94, Manchester-road, Warrington, was killed in action Sunday, September 3rd. He had bitn in France only a fortnight, and when he fell, as the result of being struck by a shell, he was leading his company in the first charge in Which he had taken part, having joined his battalion so recently as the Friday and gone into the trenches on the Saturday. Information respecting this young and gal- lant officer's death is, as yet, meagre, but the Chaplain has written to say that he was one of the first of many officers to fall, and that he was leading his men to the attack; while the Adjutant adds the fact that death was instant.

A diary which Captain Singleton left was 86t home by the Chaplain, and in it has been , li -"Rind an entry to the-effect that he had "just Inet George Brooke, of Latchford, who is attached ,to our , division as machine gun °facer"; and a letter received by Mrs. Brooke (Sninmerfield, Latchford) and written by the gentleman named last Thursday, says that Captain Singleton's body had just been found and he had attended his funeral.

Captain Singleton, who. was 26 years of age, Was married- last October to Miss Frances Muriel L. Clark, eldest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. George Clark, Bloomfield, She-.4(1. , An old boy of the Manchester Gra.mmo,r Sehdol, he took the Classical Honours Degree at,the Man- chester University, and subsequently the degree of M.A. For a time he was classical master at the County School, Richmond, Surrey, and the outbreak of the war found him filling the

CORPORAL J. BRIMELOW.

Killed While Out On Some Important Work.

Corporal Joseph Brimelow, of the South Lan- cashire Regiment, son of, Mr. and Mrs. Brime- low, 22, Pickmere-street; has been killed in

Company - Quarterthaster - Sergeant James Hampson, writing ttz Mr. George Dale, of Messrs. Jos. Crosfiehl and Sons, Ltd., says : "In consideration of Corporal Brimelow's con- nection with yourself and the firm I thought it only fair to inform you that our esteemed friend and comrade was skilled in action on the Bth inst., and quietly buried in a place behind the line. . . . He was out with his platoon on some rather important work when a shell from the enemy found him. . . . Out here, and at a time like this, one has little or no use for sen- timent, but must keep straight on, no matter what happens. In spite of that, however, I feel his loss most keenly, and having been associated with him both out here and in the works, it is only natural that one is saddened at the know- ledge that a friend has been removed for ever from human ken. Of his strong religious prin- ciples I 'need not speak; these he ever kept before him and fashioned his life and conduct accordingly, and although he and I have had many little arguments on this most debatable question, Joe would always -affirm that so far as he was concerned everything led him to the conclusion that his faith was not blindly placed, but rested upon One Who in the fulness of time would acknowledge and .substantiate that faith. I fancy I see him now offering a little tract to a comrade. 'Oh,' says the comrade,. I am a Roman . Catholic.' 'That,' says Joe, 'doesn't matter. Read it, and if you don't like it pass it on to someone else,'", and looking at me he said, with that quiet smile so characteristic of him, haven't one suitable for you.' " . . . Corporal Brimelow, who was thirty-one years of age, enlisted in the .early days of the war, and had been at the Front for fourteen months. He was formerly employed in the fuel depart- ment at Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons' works, and was actively connected with the Working Men's Mission, Bank Quay, being a Sunday

same battle, and afterwards succumbed to his wounds. Another brother, • Private ;Frank Heesom, is serving with the South Lancashire Regiment, while another, Fred. has to be medically examined.

PRIVATE R. P. CRANIPTOII.

Grammar School " Old Boy " Mortally Wounded.

Mr. and Mrs. P. P. Crampton, of Ellesmere- road, Stockton Heath, have been informed of the death after an action of their only son, Private Russell Petres Crampton, of the West Yorks. and Lancs. Regiment; on the sth inst. The news arrived at their hoin on Sunday, when Mr. and Mrs. Crampton were spending a shqrt holiday at Blackpool, and their daugh- ter had to wire to them the sad intelligence. Private Crampton was an old boy of the Grammar Schbol, attending that institution after several terms at the *Priory. Having decided to follow the same pursuit as his father, who carries on business as a draper in rHorsemarket-street, he was first apprenticed to Messrs. Dennis Roberts and. Sons, of Doncaster, and later he entered the service of Messrs. Corn Bros., of Sheffield, leaving them to go into the Army, in, which he served seven months. He had beep at the Front seven weeks when he was killed. Standing over six feet high, Private Crampton, who was 23 years of age, was an ideal Englishman, combining splendid physique with refinement and culture. How he died is described by a nurse stationed at the casualty clearing station: "I am very sorry to tell you that your son died here yesterday. He was brought in the day before with a very bad wound in his back. He was very collapsed and, though everything was (lone for him, he never rallied and died quite peacefully at noon

WOUNDED.

LIEUTENANT J. 11. J. lIAMPSON.

Battalion Bombing Officer Wounded

By British Bomb.

Second-Lieutenant John Henry J. Hampson, Lancashire Fusiliers, son of Mr. J. H. J. Ramp- son, 174, Padgate-lane, and secretary of the Warrington Infirmary; was accidentally wounded in France last Sunday by the prema- ture explosion of a rifle grenade. "I don't think I shall be coming to England," he writes home, "as the injuries are not serious. I have, been splattered on my face, chest, arms and by shrapnel, but my life was saved by the steel helmet, which took the greater part of the explosion, and my eyes were savesl owing to the fact that I was kneeling down. I think I had a miraculous escape from death or serious injury, as the explosion took place only a yard away. We should have left the trenches in an hour .or so, as we had just finished a six days' tour. I am, or was,. the battalion bombing officer."

Lieutenant Hampson, who is on the office staff af _Messrs. Rylands Brothers, joined the Lancashire Hussars almost immediately after the declaration of war. After eight months' training he was appointed second-lieutenant and attached to the South Lancashire Regi- ment, being subsequently transferred to the Fusiliers. He instructed recruits in new bayonet fighting., bombing and machine gun work up to the time of his departure for the

wounds in the chest and abdomen. He has written to his wife informing her that he is progressing favourably, the doctors and nurses doing everything possible for him. Gunner Mather, -who is 33 years of age, enlisted in November, 1914, and has seen eleven months of active service. He is an old boy, of the Wycliffe School, and was formerly employed by Messrs. Randles Bros., tanners, Tanners-lane.

Ernest now receiving treatment for wounds at the Lord Derby Hospital, Winwick. Ernest made use of John's name last week on his arrival at Arpley Station. Surprised to find himself in Warrington, as lie was being taken to the ambulance outside the station, he asked anyone if they knew Jack Waywell, the foot- baller. "Yes," said one. "Well, I'm his brother Ernest: On and tell my parents, Mr. and Mrs. Thos.,Waywell, of 88, Folly-lane, that I am here wounded, but getting on finely." The man became a special messenger at once. Though it was too late when he arrived for the parents to go to see their wounded son, they were away first thing next morning. Private Ernest Waywell is in the Rifle Brigade, and is aged twenty-one years. Before enlisting he was employed at Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, Ltd. His brother, John, has been out in France for a long, long time, but has not neglected sport, and has had the satisfaction of winning two boxing competitions and a 220 yards race amongst some very keen competitors out there. Another brother, William, is again in France after having been invalided home for eleven months. Another .brother, Private Frederick, died of spotted fever on Salisbury Plain last year.

PRIVATE S. ROWLAND.

Laid To Rest Just Behind The

Firing Line.

Tbrough the agency of a letter written by Chalplain, Mrs. Rowland, of 0, Eldon-street, was informed that her husband,. Private Samuel Rowland, Loyal Noith Lancashire Regiment, had been killed in action. The Chaplain, Rev. Reginald French, wrote as follows: "I very much regret to have to inform you that Private S. Rowland was killed in action on Friday .last. 13eath was instantaneous. God has called you to make the supreme sacrifice for your country. I pray that He Will send His Holy Spirit, the Comforter, to support you dur- ing this dark time. We laid the body to rest in a military cemetery, not far behind the firing line—a fitting soldier's grave." - Private Rowland, who was the son of Mr: and Mrs. Rowland, 53, Sharp-street, enlisted on the outbreak of war, but, after seven months' train- ing, came back to Warrington to work on munitions for twelve months. He had been in France about five months, was educated at Hamilton-street School, and formerly Worked for the British Alaminium C 0.,, Ltd. He leaves a widow and fiVe. children.

PRIVATE Ni. T. CROZIER.

Youngest Of Four Brother's Crushed By Falling Trench. Though only eighteen years of age, 1 Private Miles Thomas Crozier, South Wales 'Borderers, son of Mrs. E. Crozier, 16, Crossley-street, has been on active service for twelve months. He Was at Loos and is at present in the East. A shell recently hit the top of the trench in which he was on duty. He was lucky, he says, no shrapnel hitting -him, but the earth fell on him and damaged his ribs on the left side. His injuries, he informs his mother, are slight, so that she has no need to worry. Private Crozier is the youngest of four bro-

thers who are serving their country and he is the fiest to be wounded. He is an "old boy" of Beamont Council School where he won a certificate for swimming, and subsequently he was employed as a moulder by the Richmond Gas Stove Co. at Grappenhall.

hall, who reside at 61, Dudley-street, have been informed that their son, Rifleman T. D. Bram- hall, King's Royal Rifles, has been seriously wounded. The letter ran as follows:

, "I am writing on behalf of your son, Rifle- man T. D. Bramhall, who is wound, d. I regret to inform you that he has lost his left foot. The doctor found it necessary to take it off to save his life. I am pleased to say, he is pro- gressing , satisfactorily, and is very happy and cheerful, and says you must not worry -about' him, as he will be all right." ===== 0002601_19160916_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
CT E 3

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC.

What's this I hear about our wounded at Brookfield? Something about an individual effort to swell the hospital funds.

I heard some time ago that amongst their number they possessed some very good talent.

Perhaps it's to be a concert with the Drill Hall as the rendezvous. Whq knows? -- One never knows, does one?

That tenor singer whom we very often hear giving vent to his feelings when we happen to be passing won't half be in the limelight.

Then there are the nurses. If they are in- cluded there ought to be something done, for some of them can sing, I can assure you.

All our hopes are crashgd to the ground. The "local cruiser" has been removed (with difficulty) from the banks of the lake.

It is now safely housed in a large shed some- where in the district, and the local painter is still engaged upon it '

He certainly has improved its beauty.

By the way. Do you remember that Zepp. being brought down in Salonica? Well, we have a piece of it even in Lymm.

A small piece sent over as a relic by a local soldier was inspected by the Volunteers at the Drill Hall the other evening.

It took the form of a wisted piece of aluminium.

Nothing arresting to gaze upon, but certainly very interesting.

An important question: Are the seats placed in the new Lever-road exclusive to wounded soldiers?

A little enlightenment on the matter would greatly oblige a few doubtful sympathisers.

Now that the possibilities of public boating are considerably reduced, fishing is again the chief attraction at the lake.

Large crowds can be seen daily pursuing their favourite pastime.

It is rumoured that •it is here that our wounded heroes make some very fine catches.

I suppose it all depends on the kind of "bait" they are in the habit of using.

Baiting is allowed and not barred, isn't it?

In some places baiting is barred, but—er— well, they must be over military age. 'Nuff said.

Walking down the village the other day came across Rifleman Peter Holt wearing his gold braid. He certainly looks none the worse for his military exertions. I hear that he re- turns to France at a very early date.

Some people are not yet aware what the gold braid means.

Every piece of gold braid worn on the right sleeve of " Tommy's " tunic indicates •the number of times he has been wounded.

CRUEL RUMOUR.

Private George Drinkwater Not killed

Or Wounded.

Private George Dripkwater, South Lancashire Regiment, who, a few weeks ago, was officially reported wounded, is quite safe and sound, for last week his wife, who resides at Fox Cover cAottages, Rush Green, received the following communication from the Officer in Command -of Records:—

"Madam,—l have great pleasure in inform- ing you that the report of your husband being wounded was an error. Information has now been received from the War Office that no casualty has occurred to him." r During last week an ugly ramour had got afloat in the village to the effect that he was killed and caused considerable anxiety to his relatives. If the people who, start such rumours would only think what trouble they cause there would no doubt be fewer of them.

HOW TO STOP COCAINE DRUGGING.

Specialist's Common Sense Advice.

"The terrible habit of taking cocaine, -morphia, opium, chloral, &c., is in nearly every instance primarily due to a weak or impaired nervous system. These drugs, however, do not restore the depleted nervous vitality. They are mere palliatives which by exciting or t epressing the nervous system lead to a false sense of com- fort and well-being—and then the drug habit, with its terrible conse4uences, is formed. All this can be prevented, and even the terrible drug habit itself overcome, by feeding the starved nerves with the phosphoric elements, the lack of which is the cause of all nerve troubles, weakness, insomnia, lack of energy, lc. For this purpose physicians and chemists now generally advise taking with each meal a five-grain tablet of pure Bitro-phosphate, a -concentrated food phosphate which is immedi- ately converted into living nerve tissue. The regular use of Bitro-phosphate, which, by the way, costs only about twopence a day, has a most remarkable effect on the whole system. Sleeplessness disappears, for the nerves become strong and steady, brain fag ceases, endurance and strength are doubled, and dull hopelessness gives place to a bright and cheery optimism and confidence. Which explains why I invari- ably prescribe Bitro-phosphate instead of drugs to the weak, nervous and sleepless as well as to the hopeless victim of the drug habit." IMPORTANT.—Bitro-phosphate can now be ob- tained locally from H. M. Ashton, 63, Sankey- street, at 2/6 per flask, containing sufficient tablets for two weeks' continuous treatment.

MILITARY TRIBUNAL.

Chairman, Mr. T. J. Ridgway.

Representatives: Board of Agriculture, Mr. Newton Worthington; military, Mr. F. E. Evans.

Buyer and Seller's Case Dismissed.

A buyer and seller of cheap goods appeared to ask for exemption on the grounds of national interests and serious financial hardships. Mr. G. L. Welford (solicitor), who appeared for applicant, stated that his client had been in business for twelve years. His work consisted of buying goods from the manufacturers and submitting them for sale to the public at a very low price. He had to visit the markets in six towns each week, and in one case his stand was next to one occupied by an Austrian. His business expenses averaged about .22 10s. weekly. •He was not physically fit for the Army and his 'wife was. not in a fit condition to earn her own living. He (the speaker) made a very straw appeal in this case, for applicant had good stalls and if he was called to the colours wluin he came back he would have tr, begin business afresh. Two sears ago his client applied for a stand in a .certain market and he had only just got it. He understood that two men of the same 'lrade had been granted exentption in Warrington. •

- Has your client been examined by thw.Medi- cal Board?—No; but he has a certificate from his own doctor. My client has three children, all under ten years, and 17s. 6d. would not be sufficient for them to live on.

A Member: I think .you have made a mis- take.—Oh, yes; I Was thinking of the wife alone. My client has to go, in some cases, even to the actual manufacturer to get his goods, and I believe that is a very difficult .thing. One Tribunal has decided to grant all one-man businesses conditional exemption. ' The Chairman: Yes; and Lloyd George wants all the men he can get.—Application dismissed.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
Farmer For Son.

A farmer who asked for the exemption of his son stated that he himself was unfit for heavy work, and he was the only man he had and it was impossible to carry on without him. His farm consisted of 56 acres, ten of which were pasture land. The remainder was arable. He had six horses, three of Which were colts. He also had a small milk round, which his daugh- ter looked after. Have you not been able to get another man?— No; I have tried to get extra men for the harvest, but have been unsuccessful. Have you ever tried women?—No; I don't think that they Arti any good. The Chairman: Oh, they are in some places! Conditional exemption until November Ist, subject to joining the V.T.C.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
What About the Sale?

A farmer who made a second application for the exemption of his son stated that his farm contained 84 acres, 34 cows and six horses. He had six eons and three daughters. Three sons worked at home, and the others had farms of their own. Of the sons at home, one was married, one had been rejected by the Medical Board, and the other was the one appealed for, who looked after a milk round which took him all morning to get through. In the previous application the father stated that he was going to sell up this September. The Chairman: Well, have you fixed your sale -up yet?—No, sir. _ _

How many men did you say you had on the farm?—Three eons and one boy. ' Have you tried a woman on the milk cart yet?—No,

- The Military Representative: Would you not like one of your sons to serve?—One has to go in October. I,do not know how he will go on. Adjourned until October 13th for the son to go before the Medical Board. Dairyman's Liabilities.

A dairyman (36), married, applied for abso- lute exemption on the grounds of serious hard- ships. He stated that his whole life savings were invested in the business, and during the last three years he had lost two horses. He sold 63 dozens of milk weekly and it was all done from a cart. This was the second appli- cation. In the previous one he stated that he had £2O of debts due to him.

Have you recovered any of your debts yet?— No, sir.

How's that?—They are increasing. I should watch that.—Temporary exemption until December Ist, subject to joining V.T.C. Farmer Asks For A Month, and Gets It.

A farmer who appeared for a youthful assistant, aged 18, stated that his harvest was just commencing and he would like the boy to stay until it was gathered in. The boy drove a pair of horses and could do any work con- nected with the farm. He asked for a month.— Temporary exemption until October 20th, the Chairman remarking that they were pleased with the way in which he had conducted his case.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
Baker and Confectioner's Vanman.

. A kcal baker, provision dealer and confec- tioner, who made a second appeal for a van- man, stated that before the war he employed five men and one boy, and at the present time the man appealed for was the only male driver he had.—Adjourned till October 13th for Medical Board's decision. • A Peculiar Case.

A peculiar case was brought before the meeting, in which a rejected man, who had lodged an appeal. had been taken to the colours. Ie appears that the local military authority knew nothing at all- about the man being rejected; as his rejection papers had been sent to Frodshan; When his employer lodged an appeal the military authority disallowed it, on the grounds that it was lodged too late, and Called the man to the colours. Although the Mistake was soon found out the man ha& not yet been. released. The Chairman: Why wasn't he released? It appears to me that he had done all he could. Mr. Worthington: When a man once gets into khaki he cannot be released only by the Army Council. The Chairman: But what I want to know is why the Array Council have 9ot released him. The military representative was then re- quested to inquire into the matter.' [Here the Chairman retired and Mr. R. 11. Gotobed was chosen his deputy.] Skip and Hamper Makers Hard Pressed.

A local skip and hamper maker, who made a second appeal for a man, stated that at, the present time he could find work for ten men. He ,had only the man appealed for and he could not replace him, as it took seven years to learn the trade. "I have that much on hand that T- wish some of you would come down and give us a lift," commented applicant. Whereupon the military representative retorted that he was under the impression that it -took seven years to learn the trade.—Adjourned until October 13th for Medical Board's decision.

THE EXAMINERS SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 16, 1916.

BEECHWOOD.

How Lymm Accommodated Ninety Belgian Refugees.

VILLAGE TRIBUTE OF ADMIRA- TION.

Presentations to a lady and two gentlemen who have done good work in connection with the Belgian Refugee Home at " Beechwood," Lymm, formed special features of a meeting held in the Assembly Room of the Conservative Club on Wednesday evening, called for the purpose of receiving the Hon. Treasurer's statement of accounts in connection with the home.

Mr. Alfred Watkin (chairman of the Execu- tive Committee) presided, and was supported by Vice-Chancellor Dudley • Stewart-Smith, Mr. Frank Clarke (Sir William Lever's agent), Mr. T. F. W. Dixon (treasurer), and Mr. F. E. Evans (secretary). Mr. Watkin's Eulogy of the Workers. •

The Chairman said they owed their thanks to the Matrons, Dr. BUrrowes, Dr. Clement Van Heterin, Mrs. Parmiter, and Mr. and Mrs. Gavin Cook. The last-named stepped into the breach at .a very critical time, while Dr. Van Heterin had done very valuable work. The work which Dr. Burkowes had done for the home was only a small part of the honorary work whiCh he had performed, and he (the speaker) hoped that when • the blessings of peace were restored Dr. Burrowes would receive some Government recognition for the honorary work he had done in connection with the hospital and other places. (Applause.) He thanked all the ladies and gentlemen who had done anything for the home, and assured them their efforts would never be forgotten. (Applause.) The Generosity of Lymm.

Mr. F. E. Evans (lion, secretary) presented his report, which said that through the kindness of. Sir William Lever, Beechwood was opened as a Refugee Home on October 25th, 1914. it was closed on April. 286, 1916. During that period of eighteen months ninety refugees passed through the home, with a weekly aver- age of thirty-four in residence. The magnifi- cent sum of £1,507 19s. 6d. had been subscribed in Lymm and the immediate neighbourhood, and the average cost worked out it 11s..ed. per head Per week. The Executive Committee had been summoned to thirty-four meetings, all the members attending most regularly, evincing a keen desire to do all that lay in their power to make the guests comfortable and happy, and they in turn received valuable assistance from the house, clothing, and furnishing sub-com- mittees. It was a pleasure to report that only a few of the guests were unsatisfactory, and they were sent back to the Central Committee in London. Owing to the multitude of appeals upon the public on behalf of our wounded sol- diers, Serbia, and in ether directions, the com- mittee found it was not possible to continue the work on the original scale, and as. the upkeep of a large residence like Beechn-ood for a smaller number was not commensurate with the expenses tkat would be entailed it was decided to close the home in April. Fortunately with this decision there was happily another change. The majority of the male refugees had made themselves conversant with the English lan- guage, and those who were capable were found work, and se were able, in great part, to main- tain themselves. The remainder, a few women and young children, were kindly offered hospi- tality by, and transferred to, the,,Glasgow City Refugee Committee. The committee desired to place on record the services of Miss Healey and Mrs. Dorling, who ably acted for different periods as hon. matrons, and latterly Mr. and Mrs. Gavin Cook, who most efficiently looked after the internal management of the home. The liberality of Lymm people was proverbial, ' and they had shown a tribute of admiration and gratitude for the stand and share Belgium had taken in this terrible war. Each subscriber was more than fully repaid in knowing that they had rendered material assistance to a few of the sufferers, and as the late Lord Roberts said, "God grant that there may yet be in store for them a bright and prosperous future and a per- manent place in the van of civilisation.'b Refugee Appreciation and Gratitude. ,

The following letter, received from the War Refugees Committee in London, was read "Will you kindly convey to your subscrAbers the warmest thanks of my committee for their generous donations on behalf of the Belgian Refugees? I 'am also directed to offer to your committee the appreciation and gratitude of the War Refugees Committee for the splendid work which has been accomplished in Lymm." The Money: How Raised and Spent.

The Hon. Treasurer (Mr. T. F. W. Dixon) read the statement of accounts. The total in- come amounted to £1,507 19s. 6d., this being made up of the following amounts: Subscrip- tions and donations, £1,357 is. 2d. • collecting boxes, A:11 4s. 7d.; sale of old cloths, &c., 25.; garden produce sold, £l3 14s. 6d.; Refugees' contributions, £7O 16s. 10d.; telephone refund, £.3 3s. 5d.; bank interest, £6 3s. Id.; proceeds of sale of furniture, £37 13s. 11d. The payments totalled £1,491 19s. lid., leaving a balance in hand of £l5 19s. 7d. The amount was made up of the following items: Provisions, £Bl2 Bs. 3d.; household and medical sundries, £56 7s, 2d.; coal, coke and fuel, £llO 10s. 11dt; garden seeds, implements, &c., £ll 58.- 5d..; fur- nishing, 149 7s. 4d.; clothing, &c., £52 4s. 6d.; garden wages, £153 15s. id.; house wages, £2B 12s. 10d.; sundry expenses, including Refugees' -travelling expenses, repairs to property and to electric lighting plant, telephone, &c., -£lB9 us. 11d.; stamps, stationery, printing, &c., £27 16s. 6d.

The Chairman, in thanking the treasurer, said he could not do better than read an extract from the auditor's report, in which he said he was pleased to be able to say that on presenting the interim account his remarks as to the effi- ciency of the book-keeping were again justifieck Owing to the excellent way in which the accounts had been presented to him and the vouchers filed, he had been able to complete the audit with a minimum of time and trouble.

The first presentation was made to Mr. and Mrs. Cook. In asking Mrs. Cook to accept a silver salver, Mr. F. Clarke said they had had their troubles and trials even in that home, but they overcame them all very nicely. There was a time when they came to a crisis, but Mr. and Mrs. Cook stepped into the breach very bravely. They smoothed over all difficulties and differ- ences, and were most economical in the expendi- ture of the money in the house. (Applause.) In Recognition of Services Rendered.

Accepting the present on behalf of herself and husband, Mrs.. Cook said they „would treasure and appreciate the gift. in memory of the happy days spent at Beechwood. They were pleased to do they could for the Refugees. (Applause.) Mr. Clarke then presented to Dr. Burrowes a wristlet watch and travelling bag, saying the services which the doctor had rendered had been niost valuable.

Dr. Burrowes accepted the gifts,- and replied most effectively. He was very grateful for the way they had rewarded his small services, which it had given him great pleasure- to render. It had, been a pleasant little task for him. The final presentation was made to Dr. Van Heterin. It consisted of a silver inkstand, and, in presenting it, Mr. Clarke thanked the Doctor for his valuable assistance at the home. In return, 'Dr..,lleterin thanked all for the beautiful preSent and for their kindness to his fellow people. It was, he said, a real pleasure to help in the work. (Applause.) The Chairman said they could not have done all this good work if it had not been for the generosity of Sir William Lever. It would not have been satisfactory if Sir William's repre- sentative (Mr. Clarke) had not been a man with such a warm heart. It had been a pleasure to work wail-him. (Applause.) A vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman for presiding. It was decided to hand over the balance to the Red Cross Society. It was proposed to give Mr. J. Thomason, the auditor, some ,remunera- tion for his serviees, but that gentleman de- clined, saying he would like the money to be handed over to the Red Cross Society.

It might also be stated that the money for the purchase of the presents did not come out of the subscriptions to the home, but was specially contributed.

PRIVATE THOMAS READ'S BIBLE.

Found In Communication Trench And Sent Home.

A reminder has just come to hand, of the death of Private Thomas Read, Cheshire Regi- ment, who was killed at the commencement of the great advance. While working in a communication trench shortly afterwards, Lance-Corporal Townend, a member of the Royal Engineers, found a portion of a .soldier's kit, and in it was a Bible, which belonged to the late Private Thomas Read. On making inquiries he learned that the owner had been hit, and, being unable to hear anything more as regards his whereabouts, he followed a re- quest contained in one of its pages and sent the treasured belonging home to the late soldier's parents at Rush Green, accompanied by the following letter : "Dear Madam,—While I was at work ,in a communication trench at a certain amount of kit buried in the soil, which contained the enclosed Bible. I kept it in the hope of coming across someone who knew where he was; then I heard that he was wounded, but could get to hear no more. I am sending you this, hoping that you have heard from him, for I think he must have been hit and carried away. In the confusion no one is quite sure *hat happens. I picked the Bible up just after we had started the offensive again, and I knoi,y that anyone hit has no time to trouble about personal belong- ings. Seeing the reqpest inside, I thought the best thing to do would be to send it back home to you. Trusting it reaches you all right, I remain, yours sincerely, Lce.-Corpl. TOWNEND." Corporal Townend's action in this case is a title example of a Britisher's feeling towards his comrades.

PRIVATE F. I. DUNN.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
Baxter-street, has been so seriously wounded that it has been found necessary to amputate his left leg. He is. now in hospital in Birming- ham, progressing satisfactorily. His last letter home before he was wounded was written on August Bth, in which he said he was in the midst of the push, and that his regi- ment were "going over the top" that day. lie added he would "do his best."

Private Dunn, who is forty-one years of age: enlisted at the beginning of October last year and went to France in February. He was educated at Wycliffe School, and previous to enlisting was employed at the Corporation Car Sheds as a tram overhauler. He had worked there since the inauguration of the tramways in April, 1902, and was highly respected by all his fellow workmen.

POT V. KETTLE.

Soldiers Overlook The Lighting Restrictions.

Of the several defendants summoned to the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday for in- fringement of the lighting regulations, the one in which a soldier figured proved the most in- teresting. Helen Allen, of Eastford-road, Walton In- ferior, had been summoned, but Private George Berry, who helps to guard a bridge over the Manchester Ship Canal, came and declared that he and his two comrades who lodge at the house were responsible. The ',Clerk (Mr. Henry Greenall), therefore, amended the summons, substituting the soldier's name for that of the lady. In mitigation of the offence, Berry said that he and his mates had just got into their bed- room after being 24 hours on guard. But for a button falling off the tunic of one of them the light would not have been turned up. As it was; it was only up for the space of thirty seconds. He regarded the police's prosecution of them as over zeal in the exercise of their duty. The Chairman (Colonel A. H. Talbot): It is no use pot Calling kettle black. The police stated that the light was exposed full for five minutes at least.

A fine of 10s. was imposed, and Berry tendered a Treasury Note in satisfaction thereof. Similar fines were imposed on Henry Law, of 17, Alfred-street; A. M. Kerr, Walton-road; Annie Dutton, 90, London-road; Walter Massey, 39# Ellesmere-road; and A. F. Kershaw, 37, War- burton-street, all of Stockton Heath.

PAD GATE.

Wesley Guild.—The monthly devotional meet- ing of the Wesley Guild was held on Monday evening, when a paper was given by Miss J. Ward on "Francis Joseph Thompson and his poems." ffi was a Lancashire man, and was born in Preston in 1859 and died' in 1907. In religion he was an ardent Roman ;Catholic, but was a victim of the opium habit. Quotations were given from his poems, but special emphasis was given to his poem entitled " Tha Hound of Heaven." In this he, depicts Christ as the hound following, the soul in love, He being the " tremendou.s lover." The meeting was presided over by' the• Rev. A. J. Morton. Miss J. Stewart was the soloist and Mrs. Cottrell the pianist.

Boy Scouts.—The members of the 2nd Troop of Padgate Scouts paid a visit to the Frodsham Troop on Saturday. A number of the boys, under the charge of the Scoutmaster (Mr. W.- E. Plinston) travelled by train from Bank Quay, whilst others who were fortunate enough to posses 4 bicycles had a very enjoyable cycle ride, meeting the train contingent at Frod- sham Station The boys marched up the hill- side and "on to a place which is well-known as JacoVs Ladder Here they met the Frodsham Troop, and scouting operations were performed. The boys were formed up in two sections, ,the object being one section to defend the Union Jack and the other to attack and Obtain possession. The hillsides provided splendid cover for the operation and much skill was I shown in the direction of the various sections, resulting in a victory for the attackers. Tea was afterwards served in an old quarry on the ' hills, about three miles from the yillEge, after which the boys were left to proviae their own

RECOGNITION !

The Debt We Owe To The Late

Sergeant Brookes.

By S.D.M.

Now that we are at last getting on the right path and have begun to give our local heroes what they so rightly deserve—public recogni- tion—let us pull ourselves together and see what can be done to remedy the great wrong that we did to the late Lance-Sergeant Henry Brookes, our Russian decoration •hero. For conspicuous gallantry, devotion to duty, and heroic ambulance work, this gallant soldier was awarded the 'Coveted Russian medal and made a member of the Order of St. George (2nd Class); He was the first local soldier to bring a war honour into the village.- Surely it is not the way with Lymm people to ret such magnificent things as these to come and go un- noticed. The way in which he won the honour will for ever live in our memories, and even the smallest child at school who knows any- thing about the war and how our gallant lads have. responded can relate to you the way in which Sergeant " Harry " won the great honour. _ _

===== 0002601_19160916_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
The Story of a Brave Soul.

The story is simply told. Sergeant "Harry" went to France as stretcher-bearer, and in this capacity held one of the most important posi- tions in his company. To be, a successful stretcher-bearer one has to be "hard," intelli- gent, and brave, and in Sergeant " Harry " the Lymm lads had a man that possessed all these good qualities combined. His devotion to duty knew no bounds, and danger was to him an unknown thing. . . .

Fancy yourselves for one minute in the trenches of Flanders-; a bombardment h'as just begun and shot and shell are *ring about like hail. Suddenly, out of the tumult and din of battle, -comes that arresting cry—" Stretchers." There is a scuffle down the trench, and then, before you can say "Jack Johnson," out goes our gallant hero, carrying hope and comfort to his unfortunate comrade. According to' the tales of his comrades home on furlough, such scenes as this were a daily occurrence. With- out any thought of his own personal safety if a comrade was in danger Harry would be there, and many are the times when, through his own individual bravery, a' mother's son has been saved from an unknown grave. _ _

When ;we got to know that he had been awarded the coveted honour we were not at all surprised, for all along we had been well in- formed as regards his doings "out there." Such continual acts of bravery could not fOr ever remain unseen. -What was done for him? What was done to show him that Lymin appre- ciated the honour that he had bestowed upon us? Putting all in a nutshell, all he received was a letter of congratulation from the Trades- men's Association. That letter will for ever remain as a priceless treasure in the possession of his immediate relatives. Shortly after win- ning the honour he came over on a short fur- lough, and although we had as great a hero as ever lived in our own midst we did not realise it, and allowed him to go back without the slightest recognition. Little did we think that was his last time, in Lymm. He had not been back many hours when he himself fell a victim to the treacherous enemy and received a wound that cost him his life. Bravery was engraved in him, and he showed it to the last, for when his fellow-stretcher-bearers came to dress his wound he sat up and actually instructed them how to go about it.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Those Left Behind.

What followed remains a blank—Sergeant "Harry" passed away. His pals "out there" lost a great comrade and we at home lost st; real hero. He has gone, 'tis true, but 'what I want you to remember is he left a wife and child, and if you want to recognise him now, and show the world that you appreciate the honour that he has bestowed upon you, you canl do it through those he loved so dearly. I hear that his wife is shortly to receive his medal, so let us make it a double event and show her and the community at large that Lymrn appre- ciates what her noble sous have done for her. I should ask our worthy Council to pull them- selves together and organise a house-to-house collection. Just appoint, say, two attractive young ladies in each ward to go round and put the scheme before the public, and if I know anything about the people of Lymm they will have nothing to fear as regards the result. Do not delay it any longer, for it is -now con- siderably overdue. Remember that it will not be charity—it fill only be a suitable way of showing our greatest gratitude.

Children's Effort For Wounded Soldiers.—A collection has been made at Padgate C.E. School this week on behalf of Mr. Stewart Royston's Walking I Stick Fund. The children have been most enthusiastic in the idea of help- ing this deserving local fund, and have exer- cised much ingenuity in raising, money, such as Making and selling toffee, cushions, scent satchets, pincushions, &c. As a result the splendid sum of £9 15s. Rd. was sent to Mr. Royston on Wednesday. In addition six walk- ing sticks have been given. The principal con- tributors were: Olive Povey, El 12s. 3d. • Frank Povey, El 10s.; Edith Jump and Mabel Allen (jointly), El 6s. 4d.; Margaret Wright, 155.; Harry Antrobus, £1 25.; Eva Antrobus, 13s. 6d.; Hilda Mellor and Mary Unsworth (jointly), 125.; Arthur Oldham, 7s. id.; Ernest Haigh, 65.; Irene Oldham, ss. Bd.; and May Tunstall, 3s. Cyril Longshaw brought three walking sticks. The children decided by resolution that the total proceeds should be devoted to the Lord Derby Hospital, where, it is understood, sticks are urgently needed.

Buried Twice: Private Denham's Somme Ex- periences.—Mr. and Mrs. Richard Denham, of Padgate, have received news that their son, Private Sydney Denham, of the First Canadian Division, has been wounded .in the Somme battle. Writing from the Canadian Hospital in France, Private Denham says :—" I am at pre- sent at Boulogne and am getting on pretty well. I got the shrapnel taken out of me yesterday (September 10th). I am booked for England, but do not know just at present when I will be moved. It is rather hard lying around, but it is jake ' to be under clean sheets once more and away from that old firing line. I tell you the old Somme was just a little too warm for me.' I was buried twice, a shell burst on the ground just in front of us in the afternoon and covered me all over with earth and a kind ot luminous dust, and then the one at night fisnd me for home. I have two wounds below the knee, ,two above, and one in the back. The doctors and sisters are very good and very patient, and it is jake.' Well, I hope the next letter I write home will be in England. So best love till then."

Wright, Mr. and Mrs. Alfred Earl an° family, Mr. Walter Bent, Mr. Joseph Bent, Mr' and Mrs. Sankey and family (Warrington). RUNCORN TRIBUNAL REFERENCE.

Sympathetic references were made at Thurs- day's sitting of Runcorn District Military Tribunal to the death of the late Councillor Britch, member of that body, and the customarY votq of condolence was passed. The Chairman (Mr. C. E. Linaker) said Mr. Britch was with them when they met last. Re was a member of the District Council about ten years, and he was always very attentive to his duties and took a great interest in the work. He was a kind-hearted and very helpful colleague in administering the affairs of the dis- trict, having regard always for the ratepayers, while doing his duty to the poor. He was also a very 'helpful colleague on that Tribunal. ge was sure they would have a difficulty in rePle,_e" ing him both on the Tribunal and on every -Puu" lie body on which he acted. Colonel Pilkington, as military representa- tive, asked to be allowed to join in the expres" sion of sympathy. The late Mr. Britch was,* most helpful man on the Tribunal and had given him (Colonel Pilkingt(.o- many a hint, and information which he would not otherwise have got. Mr. Baxter, representative Board of Agricul- ture. was quite sure that Mr. Britch always did what he thought was right. There was no fear or favour about him. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Antwis said the Board had lost a very useful member.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH.

Signaller H. Clark Wounded.—Signaller Harold Clark, attached to Cheshire Regiment, son of Mrs. A. Clark, Warrington-road, Pen- keth, is at present in hospital at Winwick suffering from shrapnel wounds in arm and fractured skull. He volunteered for service on January 4th, 1915, three- days after his 19th birthday, and after 12 months' training was drafted to France. On September 2nd, after nine months of hard work and thrilling ex- periences, he was seriously wounded. The shell which put him out -Of action also killed his chum at his side. This was the second ex- perience of being buried alive he had within one month. The first time he escaped unhurt, but the second time he was not so fortunate. Prior to his enlisting he was employed by the Pen- keth Tanning Company, and was amongst the earliest to volunteer.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCHMAN & FARMER.

Death And Funeral Of Mr. E. Britch, Of Appleton.

In the death of Mr. Edwin Britch, Thorn Brow Farm, Appleton, from heart failure on Saturday evening, at the age of 50 years, Cheshire farmers have lost one of their most respected colleagues. He was a son of, the late Mr. James Britch, of the Thorn Inn, Appleton, and was well-known in the district. He was a prominent leader in church work for many years. _ _ _

- The late Mr. Britch, who had lived at Tkorn Brow Farm, for 24 years, was married 12 years ago, and leaves two children. He was the vice-chairman of the Appleton Parish Council, was District Councillor for the parish of Apple, ton, and a school manager, besides being a sidesman at St. Cross Church, Appleton, a member of the Runcorn Rural District Tribunal, and chairman of the Warrington Branch of the Mid-Cheshire Milk Producers' Association. He was interested in Freemasonry, being a member of "St. Elphin " Lodge, and was also connected with the following societies: Royal Agricultural Society of England, Royal Lanca- shire Agricultural Society, Mid-Cheshire Agri- cultural Society, and Warrington and District Agricultural Society. On Sunday evening, at St. Cross Church, Appleton, the Rev. H. G. Thompson (vicar), paid the deceased gentleman a striking tribute, remarking that the news had been received with great regret, and it was the duty of the congre- gation to offer prayers for the comfort of the mourners.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Impressive Funeral.

Great respect was shown for the late Mr. Britch on Wednesday afternoon, when he was laid to rest in the yard of St. Gross Church, Appleton, The service in the church and at the graveside was conducted by the Rev. H. G. Thompson, and the Rev, the Hon. C. F. Cross, vicar of Stretton. The principal mourners were: Messrs. James and Arthur Britch (brothers)„ Mrs. Chrimes and Miss Britch (sisters), Messrs. A PICTIRISED ROMANTIC NOVEL BY ROY L.IvIcCATTELL ADAPTED PROM THE SENSATIONAL"FLYING SERIAL FILM

BY ALEC J. BRAID.

racters in this story are purely imaginary, living

HOW THE STORY GOES.

lh 1632 Sir Arthur Stanleighi banished from Eng- -land, went to Virginia. His gallantries to an Indian maid placed him in dire peril, and he was only saved from death by the fall of a meteor. He •ole.irned this phenomenon as a mark of the die- Pleasure of the Great Spirit, and the Indian tor- turers believed him. Embedded in the meteor was -a wonderful gem, and, known as The Diamond from the Sky, it became the heirloom of the Stanleighs. Prom one generation to another it went with the -Earldom of Stanleigh. When the Warwickshire branch of the family die 4, the heir was sought ainong the Stanleighs of Virginia. Two centuries after Sir. Arthur's remarkable deliverance, the eighth Earl died. The Earldom and diamond would gO to Colonel Arthur Stanleigh and his son, but a daughter was born, and the Colonel purchased the baby boy of a gipsy king, bringing up the girl in +secret. His cousin Judge Lamar Stanleigh, had a non named Blair, and when the two youths grew to- Inanhood the old feud was revived. Our story nar- rates the extraordinary adventures which befel those who wished to possess the diamond. Arthur Stan- leigh, known as "John Powell," is the false heir. Elair Stanleigh, his cousin, is assisted by Vivian Marston, an adventuress, in the recovery of the diamond. Esther is the daughter of Colonel Stan- leigh, and Quabba her faithful henchman and pro- tector.

CHAPTER XVI

and have no connection with or relation to any persona

of fire, the sound of showering rocks, a heavy fall of earth and debris that seemed to heave out, and groans and rattle /and settle—then a choking fog of smoke and silence.

Whatever had been the matter with the motor, the shock and jar of the explosion had settled and loosened the wire back in place to a proper con- tact, for when, shaking with fright and excitement, Quabba dragged Esther aboard the motor again and turned the lever, the little machine moved for- ward like a living thing, slowly at first and then gaining speed, glided swiftly and smoothly out of the smoke and so on, swifter, faster, until the hunchback and the trembling girl felt a breath of fresh air from outside blow upon them and saw a gleam of daylight. In the sunlight they told their story to the astonished miners, who went into the workings in search of Blair Stanleigh and Luke Lovell, but they were not found. Good fortune had been with them that night While Esther told her story to, the mine boss on the other side of the mountain, the defeated villains were nursing their injuries.

Whoever the fair sailor was "John Powell" knew that no skill would avail her. Unless a miracle happened she was doomed But he was not a man to stand idly by while a woman perished miserably. Pulling off his coat as he ran, Arthur plunged into the waters of the bay to save her. Risking death for a diamond. Vivian smiled in her path of peril as she saw Arthur clear the rail. Vivian Marston had chosen that path to gain her purpose, and *as content when the strong arms of the young mil- lionaire held her till help arrived. So Vivian sighed with vague contentment, and clung, seemingly unconscious, to Arthur as they were hauled into the lifeboat; and when she was borne up the companion-way and into the hand- somely-furnished cabin of the yacht's owner who had rescued her, she could have laughed aloud for sheer joy at -the success of her wild plan. The moon shone on the waters of Santa Barbara Bay. It shone upon the broad white deck of a great yacht which moved majestically across the waters. Beneath the moon, a chaplet of flowers in her hair, a clinging white gown of Grecian sim- plicity setting off her languorous beauty; reclined Vivian Marston. Near her was Arthur Stanleigh, enthralled, enraptured under the spell of Vivian's alluring charms, the sensuousn,ess of the love-song played by the orchestra, and the magic of the moon- light over all. -Under the spell of Vivian's wonderful eyes, Arthur Stanleigh, •" John Powell," millionaire now, trod the path of peril in his turn, but the path was not Plain to him, for it was hidden by deadly flowers. The flowers on the path of peril seemed sweet and fair to him, they might be such as were in the chaplet on Vivian's dark locks, for Arthur bent over those and murmured: "I would give you any- thing in the world."

An eager glow came into Vivian's dangerous eyes, a deeper flush suffused her fair cheeks. "You would give me anything in the world?" she whis- pered. "Then get me The Diamond from the Sky." "It has disappeared utterly off this earth," an- swered Arthur. "If it comes to light it may not be mine to give," and he leaned over to clasp her in his arms; but, pouting like a child denied, Vivian held him aloof. "You do not care for me," she said, with effective plaintiveness, "or you would promise." And Arthur, such was the witchery of the woman, such was the magic of the moonlight. Promised her. "The diamond shall be yours," said he.

Away in New York the magic of the moon cast no snell over Frank Durand, "King of Diamonds." He was an entirely practical person, chief of a gang of international jewel thieves. His unenviable repu- tation was the result of an accumulation of lawless deeds in many countries. More than one term of imprisonment had been paid as his price for daring hauls. Now he was a free man " working " New York, always ready to embark upon any scheme promising profit. Vivian Marston had been his assistant in more than one adventure, and he had often wondered what had become of the astute young lady. That she was engaged in no good work he was quite certain, but her whereabouts had been unknown to him and Felix d 6 Vaux—Count de Vaux—a clever young man, whose escapades were numerous. Determined to recover the diamond and not alto- gether certain in his mind that Tom Blake, the Richmond detective, was so enthusiastic in his behalf as he pretended to be, Abe Bloom wrote to the "King of Diamonds" to assist him. "Dear Durand," he wrote, "4 have been done in the eye by a smart couple of schemers: Blair Stanleigit, a Virginian aristocrat, and your old friend Vivian. Between them they have stolen , The Diamond from the Sky, a great gem set in an I antique necklet. It is a diamond of fabulous value. It was pawned, and then Vivian wore it at a ball and I lost it. But she had it again when she married- Blair Stanleigh. Fancy Vivian marrying into one of the best Virginian families. I want you to go to California and trace it."

." Well, I never!" ejaculated Durand. "Vivian is working on her own, is she, and marrying? That's a great joke. I'll take de Yalu. and see if between us we cannot do Vivian out of it. The Diamond from the Sky' sounds good. The Queen of Hearts ' can't play pranks with me."

Meanwhile the diamond had come to lla,rmaduke Smythe. Since the wreck of the coach he had wandered in the wilderness. Fearful of every sound, seeing wild Indians in every bush as night fell, the bewildered and often frightened lawyer longed for civilisation. One night he dreamed by a dying fire that a horrid savage leered/ at him through a monocle. Even as he was about to scalp the lawyer, the latter was conscious that his would. be murderer was attired in a faultless dress coat. Waking with a shriek Marmaduke fired his ever- ready gun into a marsh. Gathering courage, he crept forward to look at his dead adversary. When he investigated, there lay the corpse of his foeman, a great green frog, and there—where the pelican had dropped it—was the wonderful gem. (To be continued.)

THE PATH OF PERIL

For Esther and Quabba the path of peril led through the mountains. As their pursuers pressed nearer Quabba„ inspired by despair, noticed 146 • little clectric'engine by the mine mouth. He had out to speak to Esther as he sat himself in the ;driver's seat, for Esther climbed up and sat beside tkiol. A turn of the controllqr proved the power was on. and the little, low, heavy motor glided off like a thing of life, grinding and showering sparks from the overhead feed wires into the narrow dark depths of the tunnel. In they went into the darkened heart of the hills. After them, floundering and cursing over the ties, through the mud and water of the inine, panting, swearing, came the ne'er-do-well aristocrat and the outlawed gipsy, following the trolley's blue sparks with a determination that Would not be denied. Pate was working against the maid and man. In the inexperienced hands of Quabba the engine Stopped. Then, indeed, the dangers of the path of, -Peril grew manifold. "Me don't 'know what's the matter with him all the time now, Miss Esther!" whimpered Quabba. " Some wire loose, maybe, eh; what do you think?" " We are far ahead of them," said Esther, en- couragingly. " Even if the motor will only do this well they will not overtake us. It seems to me we ha.ve gone miles and miles through this dreadful darkness."

"She stops; she don't go no more," moaned Quabba, as the little motor ground and halted, then went forward a few feet and stopped with a jerk. _ _ _

" you think you can fix it?" asked Esther, eagerly. Quabba's courage returned as he noted in Esther's question a tone of confidence in his ability as an amateur electrical engineer. 'Ha!" he said. "plenty time I fix the organ. I End what's the matter and fix this." And he j.oraPed from the seat, and under an electric light In a wide part of the' tunnel where the motor had halted he proceeded to examine it. There was light !enough at this point to make any slight repairs, Providing Quabba was competent enough to do so. 13ut an intimate knowledge of the mechanism of ;street pianos was hardly of avail in repairing elec- tric motors. "Do be careful," cautioned Esther. "If any- 'thing should happen to you I should never forgive InYself. I have heard of men being killed tamper- 'ing with electric wires. And look!" Esther pointed Tto a sign at the side where a new heading from the ?lain tunnel was evidently being driven. For there In the electric light from the overhead incandescent 'bulb was a dingy `yet plain sign of warning that read "Danger! Blast ready." Near it was a small black box from which a plunger protruded, and to vhich coils of insulated wire were attached. Even to Esther's inexperienced eyes the purpose of the grim-looking little box, and the coils was apparent. It was mechanism to set off a blast, and the blast, as the sign warned, was ready. • Quabba realised the fact as well as Esther, and redoubled his efforts to locate the cause of the trouble in the Motor, and to 'get out of the danger rune. Then there came through the darkness behind them the sound of footfalls through the Water that covered the tunnel floor. "They are coming. Quabba," she cried. "What are we to do?"

- . The hunchback's usually kindly face took on a slit expression of murderous determination strange to Esther's eyes. "This time I kill them," he exclaimed, "Wait; you see!" _ .

Leaving his task -of searching for the trouble in the mechanism of the motor, Quabba pushed the little machine with many grunts and straining efforts 'from the dangerous spot near the blast and its warning sign. Pushing and panting, he shoved The machine a hundred yards or more down the tunnel. Then he rushed back under the electric light by the blast, and seized the battery by its leather handle, uncoiling the loops of wire, the Other ends of which were fastened deep down in the blast holes to the detonator in the dynamite. Quabba slipped back through the darkness and called to Esther in a tense whisper to come around behind the shielding bulk of the motor with him. Prom the blast battery box he carried trailed the length of wire that carried the spark of forces of nestruction. Blair Stanleigh and Luke Lovell had located them by the light upon the electric motor. They Pressed forward with exultant shouts. Then as they reached the wide space where the blast was set, Quabba, a hundred yards away, drove the plunger of the bat- tery down swift and ,hard. RANGERS BEATEN.

Warrington Get A Good Fleta

teen Points' Victory.

It was inspiring to be again on the Wilders- pool Ground on Saturday, and, with over 2,000 spectators, watching the team on which our hopes are centred defeat Broughton Rangers by 14 'points.

On the whole the game as enjoyable and there Vere plenty of exciting incidents. It was a good start at home. Of course, there is room for improvement, this being mainly due to the, absence of the best exponents of the game. Particularly in the forward line one missed the leadership of George Thomas. During the middle of the first half, when play was in rather a vague state, we could have done with such a man as he, but preferably George him- self. He was just the type of man to pull the team together wheri it was at a. loose-end and things seemed to be going wrong. Still, for a medium set of forwards the line did all right without distinguishing itself particularly. Several of them could not have been more than 19 years old, but, though young, they were fired with ambitions Despite the fact that as the game proceeded and one could detect points on which they might have acted better, one votes them as containing the material for sound men. In their evolution towards first-class forwards they have not tOo far to go., Among them, per- haps, Harmer and Cunliffe stood out the most prominently. Everyone was pleased to see Nicholas behind the scrum, and, though he was not up to his old standard, he' gave us glimpses of the skill which helped to win the cup. Young was again very active and full 'of his usual resourceful- ness, and, no doubt, if the remainder of the team will fall into line with his style of play he believes in—that a Rugby ball was made to handle and not to be kicked so much (al- though, admittedly, that is easier)—we shall witness many extremely interesting games on the Wilderspcol enclosure. ' Efficient Threequarters.

The .threequarters did very well; and, at times; tried to exploit the game in the finer arts. One noticed that towards the end of the first half and chiefly throughout the Second that they 'again fell into their old method of Adopting defensive tactics. Surely . they have learned—particularly from the displays of the Huddersfield and Wigan teams—that the best defence is a sound attack. One would like to see khis side of the game persevered with more. It 'could be rather invidious to single out for glorious mention any of the threequarters, as they all did well, but one cannot refrain from expressing a word- of praise for the young lad on the left wing, Mills, for the, way in which he adapted himself to all phases of the matph and secured one of the tries. In falling short and then rolling over to score the try .he dis- played real 'cuteness and smartness. If he has luck,,oneiventure§ for him a good future. If one says that Jolley was the Jolley as we have always known that should bp sufficient; for he showed that even the little football he had last year has kept him up to his true form. On his bearing on Saturday the committee, will have .nothing to fear in this department of the team. But special praise is due to Jolley for the way in which he Snatched the opportunity to secure the try. In Rugby a full-back putting up the points in that way is always a unique performance, Many well-known players. not have had the spirit to chance the opening and would have been forgiven—for it was none too evident, but ,Jolley's smartness and elusive- ness enabled him to beat three or four of .the opposition with a side slip, ,and then, passing the full-back, just feint in quite Fish-y • style and slip over between the stumps. Where Broughton failed particularly was in Blan, their full-back, not turning up, his place having to be occupied by Scott, of the three- quarter line, that player's place being filled by M'Graith, a forward. This re-arrangement was responsible for a deal of the lack of the organ- isation of play which makes for points.

Nicholas came over on leave from Oswestry, and was put into the team through the non- appearance of Callon, who was down to play— not because Callon was "dropped," as has beer suggested. Some misconception has arisen through a paragraph that has appeared in a daily news- paper giving the Warrington team for to-day's match. Warrington do not play to-day either at home or, away; but there is an Intermediate League game at Wilderspool.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
DETAILS OF PLAY.

Coming to the details of the play, the teams started at 3.30 with old-time promptness. From the kick-off taken by Warrington, Scott was called upon to clear his lines in the first minute and a scrim- mage near the Broughton lines was ominous. Play continued in the Broughton territory, but Robinson forced play into the home half. Dainteth received a long dropping kick in fine style, but, un- luckily, put the ball into touch. So far the play had been even and not very brilliant. Tranter had to make a Mark to save the Warrington lines from a well-placed kick by G-atley. Play was transferred to the centre and became a little more vigorous, notwithstanding the oppressive heat. Off-side in fajour of BroUghton enabled Robinson to find touch Well into Warrington quaiters, but the horde defence was very strong.

The whole of the It-angers bore down upon the home goal, but Green rushed in and saved in thrill- ing manner b _ _ _ _

Baker Breaks the Ice.

Warrington pulled themselves together and the threequarters, passed along their whole line, and Baker, gettink possession, ran well, but vras pushed into touch. The game was twenty-five minutes old when Young got possession, ran through all oppo- nents, and at the last moment passed to Baker, who dropped over the line in masterly fashion. Nicholas failed at the goal kick, but it was taken at a diffi- cult angle. Warrington continued to have the best of play, and both sides were more polished in their movements. Cunliffe three minutes later, by sheer weiglitt, forced himself over the line and scored. Nicholas failed again at goal, although the kick was from an easier position this time. Play was then carried to the Warrington goal, and a series of scrimmages took plac on the line. A. free kick to the visitors for not 'playing the ball was fruitless, though Tighe missed the goal by inches only.

Half-time: Warrington, two tries (six points) Broughton, nil.

Changing round, Broughton displayed more vim than in the first half, getting well forward. They got the better of several scrimmages, but were eventually pushed back. Their threequarters now showed, their worth, particularly Barnes ancl M'Graith, these two canting along briskly. The latter looked like scoring, but wa,s caught up in splendid style by Young. Not to be denied, how- ever, they came again, and Warrington had to thank Mills for saving the situation, when the attackers were within almost jumping distance of the line. Mills, however, could not get the ball far away, and, having it knockdd out of his hands, it was kicked over the line, and a chase after it en- sued, a home player, however, succeeding in touch- izur down. 'There was a deal of midfield play, and then War- rington gained the initiative. and Mills', who had played grandly throughout the game, raced over with a couple of defenders hanging to him. At his success there was great jubilation, not even minimised by the omission of ,Jolley to add the extra points. Shortly before the whistle blew Jolley scored in the way described above, adding the extra points if the surest of styles.

Final: Warrington, 1 goal 4 tries (14 points). Broughton Rangers, nil.

Warrington: Jolley; Mills, Dainteth, Tranter and Baker; Nicholas And Young; Harmer, Cunliffe, 'Grain, Clegg, Mathews and Ward. Broughton Rangers: W. Scott; Gatley, Eames, M'Graith and Robinson; Tighe and Barlow; Howarth, J. Scott: Quigley, Tomkins, Price and Ealse. Referee, Mr. Jones (Widnes).
