ASSOCIATION. (Lancashire Combination.)

TOWN CLUB

Turn Tables On Tranmere Rovers At Hood Lane.

The Management Committee of the Warring- ton Town Association Football Club are to be congratulated on the success which has attended their efforts in obtaining a splendid ground for the present season; and very few complaints should now be heard from visiting teams in this respect, as was often the case last season, when the players were trying to show their abilities on a very small ground. The new ground should prove beneficial to the players and also give the spectators a better opportunity of watching the Association game under the same conditions which prevail in the English League games with respect to the size of the playing area. The ground is well fenced off and there is ample space between the touch-line and spectators. •

The Town officials and players could not have iTished for a better opening match than the return fixture with Tranmere Rovers, as it will easily be remembered that the previous week in the opening game of. the season the Town got a severe beating at the Prenton Park enclosure, the result being five clear goals againsf the Town, and one, more than in the corresponding match at the Park last season. Both the 'players and the Committee were deter- mined to • reverse that result if possible and several important. changes were made in the home team, the most notable being the intro- duction of Lee (late of Eccles Borough) on the extreme right, and, with Siddall, as his part- ner, they proved a very smart and dangerous wing, Siddall very early giving Lee some good opportunities to show his skill. He was not slow to accept them and was quickly in favour with the spectators.

' Owing to some misunderstanding, Owen was not able to play in the first match, but was included in the return game, and had as part- ner Goodman (late of: Leicester Fosse): and, given further opportunities, the pair should prove a very -useful left wing, especially so with Owen, as he is very speedy .and knows Where the goals lie. He centres well from the touch-line.

' Aspinall did not show his customary form and at times was erratic with his shooting and should at least i have Scored on two occasions, when he had beat the opposing backs.

The half-backs were a hard-working trio, Leigh (of Hindley Central) Makin.. his first appearance, which was very satisfactory. Ditchfield was as hard-working as ever; whilst Whitfield, the home captain, appeared to tire in the second portion, but no doubt with a little more training .George will be all there.

Cookson was not able to play, and Leah and Millward got through a strenuous game in fine style, although Millward Mid to retire in the second half for a short time owing to leg strain, but after a little attention from the trainer he was able to play the game out. Kite did very well in goat and could not be blamed for the shots that beat him. During the run of the game he had many 'difficult shots to negotiate and cleared them in his usual style.

The visitors were minus two of the team which played the previous week, but they had very capable substitutes at hand. Their for- wards showed good understanding and did not hesitate to shoot when near the goal, Williamson and Thomas being more prominent than More- ton and Spencer. -Cunningham was a hard- worker in the centre. The half-backs were triers. Scott showed up the best. Bull and Povey both kicked well at,back, and Gallagher in goal gave a fine diSpla3r.

At the interval, the score being two goals to nil in favour of the home team, the outlook was very rosy, especially as the home lot were to have the wind and the slope in their favour in the second half. On re-commencing the visitors quickly showed they were going to fight hard, and the home defence had to be on the alert to clear their lines, and play continued chiefly in the home half, and at length Cunningham got in a low shot which Kite was unable to reach. After a spell of dilatory play Harold Lee, by a splendid individual effort, beat several oppo- nents and scored the third goal for the home team. The effort was loudly cheered by the spectators, as it well deserved to be. Then the visitors played more strongly and scored again before the call of time, but when the final whistle sounded the home team had gained the velklict by three goals against two..

Thus the Warringtonians have gained a point on the results of the corresponding games of list season.

WITH THE JUNIORS.

The Junior League campaign opened very strongly, and all the clubs were engaged. Some stirring games were witnessed.

St. Elphin's, last Season's champions, have chasiged their quarters,, and this season will play their matches on the Fairfield United ground. Last week they were successful in opening their fixtures with a fine win over the Orford Juniors by three clear goals. Dorrian \showed good form in goal for the Saints, and Vunstall was the best at back. Miller was the most prominent in the half-backs, and Jones and England were the pick of the forwards. Conley had some difficult shots to deal with in the Juniors' goal, and no blame could be attached to him for the score. Gibson, ,Roth- i well and Fitzgerald also played well for the Juniors.

The Highfield Juniors, who are new members to our Junior League, opened their -fixtures by receiving Penketh on the Tannery ground. The first half was fairly well contested, but in the second portion the visitors had all their own way, and finished easy winners by eight goals to one.

Bewsey Adult School also are new members to the League, gild were in opposition to .the Latchford Recs. (formerly Mersey Juniors), and a very evenly contested game was played. The Recs. proved the smarter •lot when near the goal, and scored twice in each half, and the Bewsey lads replied once on each occasion. J. Green, for the Recs., did the "hat trick.",

The Latchford Cadets and Howley United played before a large crowd in Vic'toria Perk. The first half was very evenly contested; and resulted in only one goal being scored. The second half proved to be a very one-sided affair. The Cadets' forwards Avere easily •defeated by the United defence, and the United were re- turned easy winners. The best goal of the match was scored by Humphries with a long shot Horn near. the tOuch-line, and all the United team played well, whilst only Jones, Greenhalgh and Williams are worthy of men- tion of the Cadets.

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

From the centre kick the visitors attacked strongly, and Moreton centring splendidly, Kite had to save good shots from Cunningham and Spencer. Mid field play followed, and each defence easily dealt with any long shots that were put in. Millward had to,retire for attention from the trainer, and during his absence the home defence was severely pressed, Kite having many shots to negotiate. Millward then returned, and the game was more evenly con- tested. The visitors again became the aggressors and showed better command of 'the ball than the home team, and after several strong attacks had been staved off, Themes succeeded in scoring the visitors' second point. After this reverse the home men applied more vim into their play, and Gal- lagher was in evidence with several smart clear- ances. Moreton, on the visitors' right, was well served by his colleagues, and after beating several opponents he gave Kite a stinging shot to deal with, which was saved at the expense Of a ,corner kick. This proved fruitless, and the home team forced a corner at the visitors' end. This was well placed by Leigh, but Goodman shot over the bar. From a throw-in the home goal had a narrow escape from being captured, Williamson shooting at the wrong side of the net when keenly tackled by Siddall. Play was in midfield when the final whistle went, the result being: Warrington\ Town, 3 goals; Tranmere Rovers, 2 goals.

ST. ELPHIN'S v. ORFORD JUNIOR::•

The following players represented the above clubs in their opening League match on the Steel-street ground last Saturday:— St. ElWhin's: Dorrian; Tunstall, Cooper; Buckley, Miller, Watson; Chisholm, England, Tither, Jones, Holden.

Orford Juniors: Conley; Doughty, Gibson; Aus- tin, Witten, Bothwell; Griffiths, Fitzgerald, Hirst, Gleave, Belshaw. Referee, Mr. It Fitzgerald. Orford won the toss, and Tither set the ball in motion. Keen exchanges were witnessed, each goal being visited without result. After ten minutes' play a brilliant run by Jones enabled Holden to open the score by heading the ball safely past Con- ley. Encouraged by this success the Saints were quickly on the aggressive, but fine defensive tack- ling by Gibson relieved, and the Juniors trans- ferred to the home end, where Tunstall proved to be very safe. Jones made several fine individual efforts for the Saints, but failed to get through, and at the home end Dorrian had to be alert to save a good shot by Hirst. Miller was very prominent in breaking up many attempts of the Juniors, and from his placing Tither broke away and forced a corner kick, which they failed to improve. Follow- ing a scrimmage in the Orford goal, England scored the second goal for the Saints Fitzgerald showed up well for the Juniors, but was at fault with his final effort. Half-time: St. Elphin's, 2 goals; Or- ford Juniors, nil.

• On resuming. the Saints monopolised the game. and Jones. Tither and England were a source of trouble to the visitors' defenders. Fitzgerald trans- ferred play to the home end, and from his centre Belshaw shot hard and straight, and Dorrian made an effective clearance. Then Conley saveci two hot shots from Watson, and during heavy pressure Doughty and Gibson kicked well. Holden and Jones combined well for the Saints, and several smart centres by the former went abegging. Chis- holm scored again for the Saints, after good drib- bling. Result: St. Elphin's, 3 goals; Orford Juniors, nil.

HIGHFIELD JUNIORS V. PENKETH JUNIORS.

On the ground of the former, under very favour- able conditions. Penketh won the toss, and Mr. J. Ford, chairman of the home club, kicked-off for the visitors. The opening/ exchanges ,were very even, but it was not long before the visitors showed their superiority and opened their score after ten minutes' play. .The home team then tried to equalise, but were easily held in check by the visitors' defence, and play was transferred to the home end, the visitors scoring again. After this reverse tile home team played more keenly, and after several attacks had failed J. Ford, jun., succeeded in scoring a well-earned goal. The score at half-time was :—Penketh Juniors, -2; Highfield, 1. The, visitors were soon in front of the home goal and registereil their third coal. This reverse had a bad effect on the home team and they fell away. The visitors added four goals in quick succession, and Pearson scored again a few minutes before time. Result :—Penketh Juniors, 8 goals; High- field Juniora, 1 goal.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 16, 1916.

MILITARY TRIBUN7ALS.

rviED:CAL BOARD.

Chairman's Caustic Comments.

Piquant remarks about the Medical Board were made at the Warrington Tribunal at the Town Hall on .Wednesday, during the hearing of a case in Which Mr. H. J. ,Westbrook (of Messrs. Robert Davies and. solicitors) repre- sented _ a draper, who appealed, on business grounds or his son, single and 21 years old. Mr. Westbrook first 'explained the indispensa- bility of the young .man, emphasising that a female could not do the work, because he did most-of the buying. Ile was really ,his father's manager, the business consisting of four stalls in the Warrington Market, two at Widnes, and three at Nortnwich, where he also had a shop. If his son were taken away he would have to close the whole or part of his business. He was the oldest existing stallholder in the Warring- ton Market, haviog had a stall for about forty years. At the outbreak of the war, and feeling that he ought to do something for his country, although physically unfit for the Army, his son went into a munition works, but his health. gave way, and Dr. Geary and a ManclAster special- ist ;dvised him to leave. That he did, and re- 'turned to his father. While he was at the munition works his brother, who was now in the Army, helped his father. Now that this one had gone, it was essential that the one appealed for should remain. Apart from the business aspect, continued Mr. Westbrook, there was also the fact that the young man was physically un- fit. -He had ,been rejected by Dr. Adams in February, when he appeared for attestation, but had now.been passed by the Medical Board as fit for general service—a task which was irqpossible for him. Unfortunately, when he submitted himself for re-examination he did not take his doctors' certificates with him, as a guide to the Medical Board. That he had been passed for general service had come as a great surprise to two doctors, whose certificates he (Mr. West.: brook) now handed in. Mr. Westbrook, there- fore, suggested that the case should be ad- journed while the young man went before the ! Medical Board again, taking with him his certi- ficates.

Captain llatliams remarked that if the man had attested he had no right of ppeal against the Medical Board.

Mr. Westbrook: Then if a man with a wooden leg had been passed he would have no right to appeal on medical grounds? Captain Mathams: If you can satisfy me that such a man has been passed the matter could then receive consideration.

Mr. Westbrook: According to reports equally absurd cases appear to have been passed for general service. Captain Mathams: If the man had attested T would submit that the Tribunal should be guided by the decision of the Board/

The Chairman (Dr. Joseph): I shouldn't like to be always guided by the Medical Board. Mr. Westbrook: He has not attested, although he showed his willingness to do so, and was rejected by Dr. Adams at the time. Captain Mathams That is all right then. If he is not attested he can appeal against the Medical Board.

Alderman Tinnion: I think conscripts seem to have greater privileges than attested ,men, and I am in favour of extending the privileges of those who have attested. The Chairman: I take it that this man will go before the same Medical Board if he is re- exaniirrd? Captain Mathiins: Not necessarily.,

The Chairman: It is to be hoped not, or they will practically stick to their guns. The doctors' certificates baying been well- studied by the Tribunal, the Chairman an- nounced that the medical evidence was so con- flicting that the appellant would be referred for further examination. "The Tribunal will communicate with the Medical Board," he concluded.

, Town Councillor's Appeal. A Town Councillor, who will be 41 years of age in February, was conditionally exempted. He owns a "free house" and does a conOder- able business. His wife has been an invalid for three years, and. a housekeeper and nurse had to be employed. Medical certificates were produced. The Councillor added that he had two children, one nearly 18 years old, who 701 Ild be called up for service soon. The other Child was several years younger. Single Men. •

Appealing for a wheelwright, aged 25 years and single (previously exempted owing to being in a starred occupation), Mr. James H. Hey- wood, of Wilderspool crossings, mentioned that thel man cycled nearly 140 miles a week to and froth work. Six of the appellant's wheel- wrights had joined thA Forces. He had now five wheelwrights (four of whom were exempt) as compared with seven before the war.

The -Chairman remarkeil that the appellant was very fortunate in having four men who were exempt. Refusing the appeal, the Chairman declared that the man was single and only 25 years and it would be vtry inconsistent of them to do otherwise. "We might as well stop sitting as allow a case like this to pass," he concluded. The case 4)f another single man, aged 21 years, was also heard, Messrs. T. and J. Ride: way, solicitors, making the appeal. He is a general clerk. It was admitted that there had been four postponements. The Clerk (to Mr. Denham, who represented the firm) : Haven't you been able to do anything yet Ittzl have secured a boy, but we are in the same position as before. Captain Ridgway has been away since Augupt, 1914. lam in your hands. For eighteen months I have worked every night and on Sundays, and if you take this man it will be worse for me. The Chairman: We have no alternative but to refuse the appeal. Mr. Denham: I quite understand the diffi- culty, gentlemen.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
Two Brothers and a Business.

A builder and contractor, aged 40 years, who has been in business on his own account for 18 years, asked for exemption. Before the war he employed between 30 and 40 men and had a turnover valued at £6,000. -;sTow he had . a man and a boy only, and his! turnover totalled £l,OOO.

The Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway): Hasn't your brother been exempted, and wouldn't there be any chance of one of you carrying the business on? We don't say which.

Applicant: We could not agree. We were with each other for twelve months, but we parted then.

The case was adjourned for a fortnight in order that applicant might be medically examined and in the hope that he would be able to make some arrangement for the carry- ing on of his business, • Grammar School Master.

The Governors of the Grammar School ap- pealed for the junior modern master, Mr. H. J. Westbrook (Messrs.' Robert Davies and Co.) stating ,their case, and having by his side the headmaster (the Rev. Horace Gray). The Governors, remarked Mr. Westbrook, considered this master indisperksable if the work Of the • school were to be carried on efficiently., " He was responsible for the specialis6d teaching of geography throughout the whole school •and half the teaching of the drawing, as well, as history. The Governors were of opinion, that he was already engaged. on work •of national import- ance, having regard to the fight for col:inner eial and industrial supremacy which would follow the war. If we were to be successful in that fight it was absolutely necessary that the boys should be fully equipped educationally. The Chairman: Geography is very much dis- organised at present. Mr. Westbrook: Yes, it wants watching very closely. _(Laughter.) _ _ _ _ --

The Clerk: You could get a "Daily Mail" map o'ouldn't you? (Renewed laughter.) The Rev: Horace Gray said that when he engaged the mastin. in January, 1915, he was given to undeotand that there was practically no sight in the right eye, and consequently was unfit for trench service. The Master: But they have now passed me for general service at Ashton. It was a very cursory examination and did not last more than a minute.

The master had also entered a domestic appeal Which was heard in private, and after- ward!" it was announced that he had been exempted conditionally upon these personal circumstances.

The Town Hall Tribunal yesterday was com- posed of Dr. Joseph, Deputy Mayor (chairman), Alderman Tinnion and Mr. F. A. Frost. 1 Musician and Postman.

Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, represented an auxiliary postman who sought exemption on domestic and personal grounds. He is a married man, -aged 38 years. Owing. to an ail- ment, which he has had for fourteen years, ho had beem advised to take up only light employ- ment. He learned the clarinet and .had an engagement in ti.:e orchestra of a local place of amusement. When he applied to attest he was medically rejected, but on recent examination before the Medical Board he was passed for general service. . Captain Matliams submitted that applicant had not put in any evidence except that he had been rejected before. The case was adjourned in rder that appli- cant should be again medically examined. Time-Expired 'Man.

. , A single man, aged 24 years' at present em- ployed in .a tannery, applied for exemption. tie had been in the local Territorials and went to France, where he had been for 12 months and 24 days. He was discharged as a time- expired man and was now called up under the Military ;Service Act. He explained that he had a mothei and sister to maintain, a brother who had .• recently been married .not giving any help in the home. Captain Iliithanis: Have you put it to him that his action is not very patriotic and that he Might do more for the family? Applicant: I put it to him when I was in France. The case was' adjourned for a week.

Lad of Nineteen to Go.

A coal dealer asked ..that his nineteen-year- old son shduld 0 exempted. Ile had five sons, one of whom" had been killed in France. Another is in the South Lanes., and two others aged nine and seven years respectively. The Chairman said the Tribunal had every sympathy with applicant with .regard to his other son being killed, but they had no alterna- tive but to refuse the .appeal. Applicant asked if all boys of 19 were sup- posed to go up, because, if so, other people had sons they were not going to part with. The Chairman said if they etame before that Tribunal they would have to part with them. Applicant said he did not object to letting the lad go, but he had consumption of one lung. He was advised to bring a certificate to that effect to the Medical Board.

TANNERS' AFFAIRS. A Big Bid For Men On Mupitions. Interesting statements concerning tanning companies' anxiety to retain their ;nen were made at the sitting ,of the Warrington Rural District Tribunal on Wednesday, when Messrs. Mortimer and Co., of Orford, appealed for nineteen men who have been debadged, the Chairman (Mr. George Mather) observing that that was the result of the combing out ptocess. Mr. W. Furness (military representative) ex- plained that a few weeks ago the Tanners' Federation entered into an arrangement with the Munitions Department and the War Office, by which certain debadging took place, which, in effect, was that men under 25 years and, in some cases, under 30, years and unmarried, should be given up. In harmony with this arrangement/the Penketh Tanning Company had 23 men debadged, Messrs. Mortimer and Co. suffered the same, and, he expected, that similar procedure would ocatir el,4ewhere in the Warrington district. What had happened Was that the Penketh Tanning Co. was not agreeable to the debadging, and took objection to the Federation making them practically a party to it. Twenty-three of their men having been de- badged, they had appealed to the Ministry of Munitions and s,ecuired no fewer than sixteen badges for redistribution among the men affected. "We are anxious to find out why it is that the Penketh Tanning Company should have had preferential treatment)! commented Mr. Furness.

Mr. Gough,. of Messrs. Mortimer and Co.. mentioned that he had written to the Ministry of Munitions, but had 'received no reply beyond an acknowledgment. An adjournment of a fortnight was made to see the result of the negotiations. Appealing for seven men, the Woolston Tannery had six conditionally exempted, the other, a single man, being refused.. A Beer Blender's Exemption.

A single man, of 27 years, who is a cellarman and blends the beer at Forshaw's Brewery, Burtonwood, was conditionally exempted. Mr. Forshaw said that the brewery, was down to \a minimum of labour. They were working with half the hands they ought to have, and every man had gone who could go. Mr. T. S. Steel, soliaitor, represented the Penketh Leather Manufacturing Company in their appeal for two men, aged 39 and 41 years, and gained for them conditional exemption.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHESHIRE SIDE.

The local Tribunal for Runcorn Rural Dis- trict sat at Dutton Workhouse on Thursday, Mr. C. E.•Linaker, sen., presiding, and disposed of a number of appeals from various parts of this widespread area.

Shepherd's Application.

Mr. H. J. Westbrook, solicitor (Messrs. Robt. Davies & Co.), appeared on behalf of a sheep grazier, residing at Stockton .Heath, an un- married man, aged 39 years. It was stated in support of his appeal that he shepherded from 200 to 300 sheep in the district and had no assistance save that rendered by two dogs. He had also sheared from 600 to 700 sheep for different farmers. A letter from Messrs. Main- waring was read in support of appellant's statements. He had been rejected on attesta- tion, but was subsequently passed for work with labour units and for regimental outdoor employment.—The Tribunal granted conditional exemption. Short Exemption for Teamsman.

Mt. William -Leech, Whitley, appealed on behalf of a teamsm.an, aged 21 years, single, who had already been exempted until Septem- ber Ist. There were 95 acres in the farm and this was the only paid man applicant employed. In reply to further questions, applicant stated that two sons, 28 and 15; were also working on the farm, the former having been granted con- ditional exemption. He had a man from the Army for a week, when he was recalled.--Ex- emption was given until October Ist. Hay Trusser and Binder.

A contractor in Stockton Heath, who carries on the business of hay trusser and Under, appealed for exemption for his son, 18 years Of age,' unmarried, who, he stated, was indispen- sable to him in carrying out a number of 'orders on hand. Applicant has two Sons in the Army, the present being the only one left at home.—ln reply to the Military Representative (Colonel Pilkington), applicant said .this son would be nineteen on the sth December.— Exemption was granted to Ist December.

===== 0002601_19160916_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
A Parish Contract.

Mr. John, H. SPensleY, contractor to the Stockton Heath Parish Council, applied for exemption of a carter, aged 34, unmarried, who .chiefly employed in the removal of house refuse in that 'district. THE KINEMAS.

"The Battle of the Bomme," shown at the Star Kinema during the first part of the week, attracted, thousands of people', and recollections of it will be very valuable at the present time, when we are studying the recent gallant advance which has been achieved. Next week also up are to have another war picture, "The Advance on the Somme." There is now show- ing a thrilling drama, "The Golden Chance." Next week there will also be shown during the first half ,a fine Lasky Co. drama, "To Have and to Hold." During the latter half we will have another drama, "The FlaAh of an Emerald."

At the Grand Electric Theatre, Bridge Foot, there is now showing "Man and His Soul," a fine metro drama, and for next week there will be "The Hard Way" and the sixteenth epifode of the "Diamond From the Sky" during- the first half, and "Peggy of the Cotton Mills" during the latter. At the Pavilion, "Her Heart's Desire" is now being screened, and for the first half of next week there will be an exclusive picture, "Salomy Jane" and the sixteenth episode of the "Diamond From the Sky." "She "—from the famous novel—will be the " star " ,picture for the latter half

===== 0002601_19160916_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
Dr. Joseph on Games and National Character.

The Deputy Mayor (Dr. Joseph), Who handed the silver cup (presented to the Association by Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart.) to Mr. A. Broady, captain of the winning team, said as on former occasions he had been impressed by the sporting character of the losing team, who were the first to congratulate the winners upon their success. The British as a rule were devoted to sport; and they observed the rules and "played the game." We applied the term "fair play " to every phase of life, a fact which produced a good effect upon the national character. The Germans did not go in for sport to the same extent—they preferred German Kultur. So far as he knew there had not been a single instance in which we as a nation in this war had been guilty of unfair play. But what was the record on the other side? They knew the manner in which Belgium, Poland and Serbia had been treated, how the Lusitania was sunk, and how Nurse Cavell and Captain, Fryatt were treated; they could not call it fair play. He mentioned these facts to show that our national games had an influence upon our national character and,made us act square even during a dreadful war such as the present. (Applause.)

NINE GUINEAS FOR PRISONERS OF WAR FUND.

At the close of the games the cup (presented by Sir Gilbert Greenall,/ Bart.) and prizes were dis- tributed to the successful competitors by Mrs. Henry Greenall in the presence of a large com- pany. _

Mr. W. Patrick (hon. sec. of. the Association) wel- comed Mrs. Greenall, and expressed satisfaction it her continuing to show her interest in the affairs of the Association. Lady Greenall was prevented from being present by a prior engagement. Mr. J. Willett, in asking the Mayor to accept a cheque for nine guineas from the Association for the local Prisoners of War Fund, said the 6am- mittee thought it would be a good plan to devote half the proceeds of the handicap towards provid- ing the many interned Warrington lads in Ger- many with food and necessaries. They had ample proof in letters from the men at the Front that whatever the Association did for them was fully appreciated. THE MAYOR AND THE NEED FOR A WORKS GROUND.

The Mayor accepted the cheque and said an organisation that supported good causes as liberally as the bowlers had done was bound to prosper. The Parr Hall workers were sending out over £35 worth of food to the prisoners weekly, including cocoa, sugar, bread, tinned meat and fruit. The aggregate weight of the consignment was threequarters of a ton, and they knew from the men themselves that had it not been for this substantial assistance many of them, particularly the weaker ones, would have gone under long since. Unfortunately, our boys in Germany are being badly fed, while we looked after our German prisoners in a proper manner— as Englishmen should. (" Too well.") In the re- treat from Mons, South Lancashire men were cap- tured in 'large numbers—larger than would ever happen again. We were now in the capturing vein, and he hoped the time was not far distant when' we should be able to deal satisfactorily with the questioif_of treatment. (Hear, hear.) _ _

He understood that the handicap entries this year numbered nearly 900 against 1,100 last year and 1,700 in the year before the war. He thought there was something wrong if such a strong body of men had not sufficient grit in them to launch out and secure a green of their, own that would always be at their' command. The offer he made to the secre- tary a few years ago to assist in the formation of a limited liability company to carry out the scheme was still oiaen. He advised the members to look around for a suitable piece of ground. to get the price fixed, and, if they raised about one-third amongst them, he would guarantee that they would get the other two-thirds lent them in shares by himself. with a view to getting a dividend. (Laugh- ter.) War or no war, the game of bowls had to go on. "It develops a kindly feeling between men," conCluded.the Aftyor, " and helps to hind them to- gether with the one aim of making the country better." (Applause.)

Mrs. Henry Greenall then handed the challenge cup (to which were added a clock and bronzes) to the winner of the handicap. PH:Ts were presented to the other successful competitors.

RE-VISIT AFTER AN ABSENCE OF FOUR YEARS. CO-OPERATIVE HALL, CAIRO STREET, WARRINGTON. - SEPTEMBER 22, 23, 25, 26, 27, 28, 29, and 30, 1916. ...SLN EIGHT-DAYS' GRAND Floral Exhibition and Sale of Beautiful Artificial Flowers Made by Blind and Crippled Girls Connected with John Groom's Crippleage and Flower Girls' Mission (moor.), will be opened 9 • in the above Hall by MRS. RYLANDS, on FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 22nd, at 3 p.m., Under the Presidency of lIIS WORSHIP THE MAYOR (Alderman Peacock), Supported by the Local Clergy and Ministers and Influential Ladies and Gentlemen. Music each Afternoon and Evening. Refreshments at Moderate prices. There will be a magnificent collection of Artificial Flowers for Table Decorations, Evening Wear and Millinery, all made of Silk, Velvet, or Best Woven Material—so Paper being used—and every flower is manufactured entirely by Blind or Crippled Girls. The Council of the Institution earnestly appeal for your help at this time of National Crisis. They do not ask for subscriptions or donations, but it is absolutely necessary that the girls' flowers should be sold, as their employment entirely depends on this. Some of the Blind and Crippled 'Girls will be at work each afternoon and evening making various kinds of flowers. ADMISSION FREE. Open each following day (Sunday excepted) from 11 a.m. to 9 p.m. The Institution has been in existence over 50 years. ALFRED TROUGHTON, Exhibition Manager.

CAPTAIN F. C. SINGLETON. Killed. position of classical master at the Sheffield Cen- tral School. Hehad formerly been ipti the Officers' Training Corps at the Manchester University, and hc at once applied for a commission, which he obtained in October, 1914. He was promoted Lieutenant in August, 1915, and Captain in April of the present year. Prior to his last Promotion he was commandant of a brigade bombing school, and with that promotion came his attachment fo the Sherwood Foresters. Some years ago Captain Singleton was a Mem- ber of the Stockton Heath Hockey Club and the Crescent Tennis Club, Latchford. SERGT:7MAJOR GREGORY. Death In Action After Twenty Years' Service. Mr. and 3/Irs. J. Hill, 44, John-street, have been officially informed of the death in action of their son, Corporal John Edward Hill, Royal Welsh Fusiliers. The Chaplain, Rev. W. T. Havard, writes, "I very much regret to inform you of the sad news of the death of your son, Corporal J. E. Hill, in action on the 25th August. He was on duty in the,trenches when a shell exploded behind him, causing instant death. On behalf of the officers and men of his regiment I extend to you our deepest sym- pathy in your bereavement." • Corporal Hill, who was educated at 'Silver-

Front ,about two months ago. He celebrated his 21st birthday in the trenches on the Friday, two days before the accident that sent him to hospital. LANCE-CORPORAL JAS. CALLAN. • Wounded.

hope I get to England, as I think I will do, for I have been hit in the muscle of the left arm. lam now down at the base hospital, which is better than being in the trenches." Lance-Corporal Callan, who is nineteen years of age, has been in the Army since October, and at the Front nine weeks. He was, formerly employed in the moulding department at Dallam Forge, and, as a boy, attended St. Ann's School. His father, Mr. James Callan, is serving in Egypt, where he has been since January. i I GUNNER J. R. LILLEY. , . One Of Messrs: Richmond's Clerkg Wounded. , Wounded, Gunner. John R. Lilley, son, of Mr. S. R. Lilley, of 13, Edward-street, Bewsey, has been conveyed to Glasgow, where lie is•getting , on as well as can be expected. The Gunner enlisted in November of last year, soon after celebrating his 19th birthday,.

leaving the offices of the Richmond Gas Steve and Meter Co., Ltd., to do so. In France since April, he had the honour of taking part in the first move forward ,in " ' The Great Push' as we call it out here," he said in a letter. He received his injuries on August 10th. Educated at St. Barnabas' School and the Secondary School, he first began .work in the drawing office at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., but left at an early date to go to the Academy-street offices of the firm named a few lines higher. RIFLEMAN T. I). RRANIIIALL. SERGEANT ARTHUR READ. \\*minded In Two Places In The Right Arm. A letter received from Sergeant Arthur Read, the popular Newfields soldier whom we reported wounded last week, states that he has been wounded in two places in the right arm. The letter was received by his parents at Newfields early in the week, and was u, welcome relief after the curt information received on the field card. Sergeant Arthur Read was educated at the local school, and worshipped at the Congrega- tional Church.' He joined the Army in Septem- ber, 1914; and chose the King's Royal Rifles as his regiment. His ability was speedily recog- nised, and in less than three weeks he gained his first step on the ladder of promotion. Six weeks after he succeeded in getting still a step higher, and was made a full corporal. He was drafted out to France in July, 1915, and shortly after- wards was promoted to his present rank. He is 21 years of age, and prior to the war was employed as a goldbeater by Messrs. Wm Wright and Sons, Ltd., the well-known local goldbeaters and refiners. He is one of a fine patriotic family, for out of four brothers, three are with the Colours, and the other one is engaged at an important munition works. He took an active interest in sport, being a member of the local Lads' Club, and a playing member of the Lymm A.F.C. His two other soldier brothers are Private Wm. Read

Bewsey lads played strongly and scored their second point. Result :—Latchford Recs., 4 goals; Bewsey Adult School, 2 goals. LATCHFORD CADETS v. HOWLEY UNITED. 1 In Victoria Park, before a fair crowd of , spectators. Latchford Cadets: Greenhalgh; Williams, Jones; I Cook, Massey, Hill; Cartwright, Ashton, Smith, Gibson, Porter. Howley United: Walters; Rowlinson, Foster; Hoarty, Miller, Hind; Potter, Boyle, Davies, Ash- croft, Humphries. The Cadets won the foss and had a slight breeze in their favour. Davies kicked off and the visitors began at a very hot pace. Greenhalgh had to save a hard shot from Davies, and from the clearance the Cadets made an attack, but were beaten off. , Even play followed, in which keen exchanges were witnessed. Then, after twenty minutes' play, Ashcroft scored the first goal for the United. The • Cadets made strcing efforts to equalise, but Walters saved finely from close range. Half-time :—How- ley, 1; Cadets, 0, ' The Howley lads had by far the best of the exchanges on resuming and play was chiefly. in the Cadets' half. In spite of the strenuous play of Williams and Jones further goals were scored for , the United by Humphries, Davies (2), Potter and Miller. The final score was :—Howley United, 6 goals; Latchford Cadets, nil. TO-DAY'S MATCHES. ST. ELPHIN'S v. HIGHFIELD JUNIORS. At Steel-street. St. Etphin's: J. Dorrian; W. Tunstall (capt.). V. Furness; A. Buckley, H. Miller, W. Watson; J. Chisholm, G. England, A. Jones, H. Gleave and 3. Holden. Reserve, A Tither. For this match the Saints are introducing several fresh players, with a view to strengthening their team. Gleave, who was captain last season, comes in at inside left, and V. Furness, late captain of the Boteler Grammar School, will play at full-back. Dorrian is again in goal, and A. Jones, of the Bol- ton Rugby team, will lead the attack. Reserve, W. Hankinson. • To meet at Bank Quay Station at 2 p.m. prompt. Train leaves 2.10. SHOOTING. FIELD FIF:ING COMPETITION BY STOCKTON HEATH VOLUNTEERS. An interesting competition- took place at the Appleton Rifle Range, the Stockton Heath Volun- teers taking part. Scplads of four men were under the command of a squad leader. They commenced the competiton in battle formation, and were con- sidered /to be under fire, advanced, extended, and took up the bring position; and under fire con- trol, fired three rounds rapid at a figure target at 100 yards, on service conditions, with the ser- vice rifle. Thirty seconds were allowed to get the shots off, being ten seconds a shot, single loading. Nine squads competed, and some interesting con- tests were witnessed. The umpire took note of de- fective drill and late shots, and deducted points accordingly. The results are as follows:— First prize: Corporal J. McDermid's Squad, Messrs. A. Hulme, W. Richardson, A. Albinson and A. H. Sanders; target score 23, deductions 6, total 17. Second prize: Sergeant E. Bradsha.w's Squad, Messrs. L. Fairclough, E. Chadwick, W. Hankey and J. Wilkinson; 21-4-17. Third prize: Sergeant C. J. Smith's Squad, Messrs. W. Howard, T. Clarke, A. F. White and J. Cox; 19-4-15. Fourth prize: Corporal H. H. Evans's Squad, 16-6-10. Fifth prize: Sergeant E. J. White's Squad; 16,-12-4. Sixth prize: Sergeant A. Mot- tram's Squad; 13-10-3. Seventh prize: Corporal A. S. Wright's Squad; 12-9-3. Eighth prize: Ser- geant J. F, Fergusson's Squad; 10-7-3, Ninth prize: Corporal J. H. clarke's Squad; 8-7-1. Platoon-Commander P. Warburton distributed the prizes, and Company-Sergeant-Major Stirrup (who ac-ted as umpire) gave some points that had not been observed during the contest. The officials who conducted the competition were: Commander. F. Warburton; umpire, Company-Sergeant-Major Stirrup; range officer. Musketry-Instructor A. Shepherd; range orderlies, Quarterniaster-Sergeant- H. B. Houghton, Privates R. M. Swinton and L. R. Rowson. The organisers wc re: Platoon-Sergeant T. T. Fletcher and Musketry-Instructor A. Shep- herd.
